**Estonia**,[^cite_note-10] officially the **Republic of Estonia**,[^cite_note-11] is a country in the [Baltic region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_region) of [Northern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Europe).[^cite_note-location-16] It is bordered to the north by the [Gulf of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Finland) across from [Finland](10577.md), to the west by the [Baltic Sea](3335.md) across from [Sweden](5058739.md), to the south by [Latvia](17514.md), and to the east by [Russia](25391.md). The territory of Estonia consists of the [mainland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainland), the larger islands of [Saaremaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saaremaa) and [Hiiumaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiiumaa), and over 2,300 other islands and [islets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islet) on the east coast of the Baltic Sea.[^cite_note-ERR_area-4] Its capital city of [Tallinn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallinn), along with the city of [Tartu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tartu), are the country's two largest [urban areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_and_towns_in_Estonia). The [Estonian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_language), of the [Finnic family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnic_languages), is the [official language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_language) and the [first language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_language) of the [majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonians) of nearly 1.4 million people. Estonia is one of the least populous [member states of the European Union](10890716.md).

Present-day Estonia has been inhabited since at least 9,000 BC. The [medieval indigenous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Estonia#Early_Middle_Ages) population of Estonia was one of the last [pagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pagan) civilisations in Europe to adopt [Christianity](5211.md) following the [Northern Crusades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Crusades) in the 13th century. After centuries of foreign rule by the [Teutonic Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_the_Teutonic_Order), [Denmark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duchy_of_Estonia_\(1219%E2%80%931346\)), [Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth), [Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonia_under_Swedish_rule), and the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire), a distinct Estonian national identity gained new momentum with the [Age of Awakening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_national_awakening) in the mid-19th century. This culminated in the 1918 [Estonian Declaration of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Declaration_of_Independence). Democratic throughout most of the [interwar period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interwar_period), Estonia declared [neutrality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutral_powers_during_World_War_II) at the outbreak of [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonia_in_World_War_II), but the country was [repeatedly invaded and occupied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_the_Baltic_states), and ultimately [annexed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_SSR) into the USSR. Throughout the *[de facto](43594.md)* Soviet occupation, from World War II until 1991,[^cite_note-17] Estonia's *[de jure](43594.md)* [state continuity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_continuity_of_the_Baltic_states) was [preserved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_Legations_\(1940%E2%80%931991\)) by [diplomatic representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Diplomatic_Service_\(1940%E2%80%931991\)) and the [government-in-exile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_government-in-exile). Following the 1988–1990 "[Singing Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singing_Revolution)" against Soviet rule, full independence was restored on [20 August 1991](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Restoration_of_Independence), and the country joined [NATO](21133.md) and the [European Union](9317.md) in 2004.

Estonia is a [developed country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country) with a [high-income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Bank_high-income_economy), advanced [economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Estonia), and [Eurozone](184391.md) membership. It is a democratic [unitary parliamentary republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_parliamentary_republic), with a single-tier local government system consisting of 79 municipalities. Estonia is among the [least corrupt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index#2020%E2%80%932023) countries in the world and ranks very high in international rankings for [education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_Index),[^cite_note-18] [human development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_Human_Development_Index), [press freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_Freedom_Index), online public services,[^cite_note-19][^cite_note-20] and the prevalence of technology companies.[^cite_note-21]

## Name

Main article: [Name of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Name_of_Estonia)

The name *Estonia* ([Estonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_language): *Eesti* [\[ˈeˑstʲi\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Estonian) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/9/9d/Et-Eesti-female.oga/Et-Eesti-female.oga.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/9/9d/Et-Eesti-female.oga/Et-Eesti-female.oga.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup>) has been connected to the *[Aesti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aesti)*, a people first mentioned by [Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Rome) historian [Tacitus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tacitus) around AD 98. Some historians believe he was referring to the [Balts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balts), while others think the name applied to all inhabitants of the eastern Baltic Sea region.[^cite_note-22] [Scandinavian sagas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scandinavian_sagas) and [Viking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viking) [runestones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viking_runestones)[^cite_note-23] referring to *Eistland* are the earliest sources known to use the name in its modern geographic meaning.[^cite_note-24]

## History

Main article: [History of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Estonia)

### Prehistory

Main articles: [Ancient Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Estonia) and [Viking Age in Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viking_Age_in_Estonia)



[Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age) stone-cist graves at [Jõelähtme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B5el%C3%A4htme)

The oldest known settlement in Estonia is the [Pulli settlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulli_settlement) on the banks of [Pärnu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C3%A4rnu_River) that dates to the early 9th millennium BC, making it at least 11,000 years old.[^cite_note-Laurisaar-25]

The earliest human habitation during the [Mesolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesolithic) period is connected to the [Kunda culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kunda_culture).[^cite_note-26] Around 5300 BCE, ceramics appear of the [neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) period, known as [Narva culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narva_culture).[^cite_note-tautos-27] The [Comb Ceramic culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comb_Ceramic_culture) emerged around 3900 BC, bringing traces of early agriculture and sophisticated religious art.[^cite_note-28] Starting from around 2800 BC the [Corded Ware culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corded_Ware_culture) appeared; this included new activities like primitive farming and animal husbandry.[^cite_note-29]



[Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age) artefacts of a hoard from [Kumna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kumna)

[^cite_note-30]

The [Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age) started around 1800 BCE, and saw the establishment of the first [hill fort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillfort) settlements.[^cite_note-31] A transition from hunter-fisher subsistence to single-farm-based settlement started around 1000 BC, and was complete by the beginning of the [Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age) around 500 BC.[^cite_note-Laurisaar-25][^cite_note-32] The large amount of bronze objects indicate the existence of active communication with Scandinavian and Germanic tribes.[^cite_note-33]

### Viking Age

The [middle Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age_Europe) produced threats appearing from different directions. Several Scandinavian sagas referred to major confrontations with Estonians, notably when in the early 7th century "Estonian Vikings" defeated and killed [Ingvar Harra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ingvar_Harra), the [King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yngling) of [Swedes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedes_\(tribe\)).[^cite_note-34][^cite_note-35][^cite_note-36] Similar threats appeared to the east, where East Slavic principalities were expanding westward. In c. 1030, Grand Prince [Yaroslav the Wise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yaroslav_the_Wise) of Kievan Rus' attempted to subjugate the [Chuds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chuds)(as East Slavic sources called Estonians and related Finnic tribes) in southeast Estonia and captured Tartu. Chuds (*Sosols*) destroyed this foothold in 1061.[^cite_note-37][^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39][^cite_note-40] Around the 11th century, the Scandinavian Viking era around the Baltic Sea was succeeded by the Baltic Viking era, with seaborne raids by [Curonians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curonians) and by Estonians from the island of [Saaremaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saaremaa), known as [Oeselians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oeselians). In 1187 Estonians (Oeselians), [Curonians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curonians) or/and [Karelians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karelians) sacked [Sigtuna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigtuna), which was a major city of Sweden at the time.[^cite_note-41][^cite_note-Tarvel-42]

Estonia could be divided into two main cultural areas. The coastal areas of north and west Estonia had close overseas contacts with [Scandinavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scandinavia) and [Finland](10577.md), while inland south Estonia had more contacts with [Balts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balts) and [Pskov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pskov_Land).[^cite_note-43] The landscape of Ancient Estonia featured numerous hillforts.[^cite_note-Magi2015-44] Prehistoric or medieval harbour sites have been found on the coast of Saaremaa.[^cite_note-Magi2015-44] Estonia also has a number of graves from the Viking Age, both individual and collective, with weapons and jewellery including types found commonly throughout Northern Europe and Scandinavia.[^cite_note-Magi2015-44][^cite_note-45]



Pre-Christian independent Estonian counties (*maakond*), c. 1200

In the early centuries AD, administrative subdivisions began to emerge in Estonia. Two larger subdivisions appeared: the parish (Estonian: *kihelkond*) and the county (Estonian: *[maakond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maakond)*), which consisted of multiple parishes. A parish was led by [elders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elder_\(administrative_title\)) and centered on a hill fort; in some rare cases a parish had multiple forts. By the 13th century, Estonia comprised eight bigger counties: [Harjumaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harjumaa_\(ancient_county\)), [Järvamaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%A4rvamaa), [Läänemaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%A4%C3%A4nemaa), [Revala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revala), [Saaremaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saare_County), [Sakala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sakala_County), [Ugandi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ugandi), and [Virumaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virumaa); and six minor smaller, single-parish counties: [Alempois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alempois), [Jogentagana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jogentagana), [Mõhu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%B5hu), [Nurmekund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nurmekund), [Soopoolitse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soopoolitse), and [Vaiga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaiga). Counties were independent entities and engaged only in a loose cooperation against foreign threats.[^cite_note-46][^cite_note-47]

Medieval Estonians' spiritual and religious practices before [Christianization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianization) were often noted for their [animistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animism) beliefs. There is [historical evidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_evidence) about [sacred groves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacred_groves), especially groves of [oak](39696.md) trees, having served as places of "[pagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paganism)" worship.[^cite_note-48][^cite_note-49]

### Crusades and the Catholic Era

Main articles: [Livonian Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livonian_Crusade), [Terra Mariana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terra_Mariana), and [Danish Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danish_Estonia)



Medieval Estonia and Livonia after the crusade

In 1199, Pope [Innocent III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Innocent_III) declared a [crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livonian_Crusade) to "defend the Christians of [Livonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livonia)".[^cite_note-50] Fighting reached Estonia in 1206, when Danish King [Valdemar II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valdemar_II) unsuccessfully invaded [Saaremaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saaremaa). The German [Livonian Brothers of the Sword](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livonian_Brothers_of_the_Sword), who had previously subjugated [Livonians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livonians), [Latgalians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latgalians), and [Selonians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selonians), started campaigning against the Estonians in 1208, and over the next few years both sides made numerous raids and counter-raids. A major leader of the Estonian resistance was [Lembitu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lembitu), an elder of [Sakala County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sakala_County), but in 1217 the Estonians suffered a significant defeat in the [Battle of St. Matthew's Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_St._Matthew's_Day), where Lembitu was killed. In 1219, Valdemar II landed at [Lindanise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lindanise), defeated the Estonians in the [Battle of Lyndanisse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Lyndanisse), and started conquering Northern Estonia.[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52] The next year, Sweden invaded Western Estonia, but were [repelled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Lihula) by the Oeselians. In 1223, a major revolt ejected the Germans and Danes from the whole of Estonia, except [Reval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reval), but the crusaders soon resumed their offensive, and in 1227, Saaremaa was the last *maakond* (county) to surrender.[^cite_note-53][^cite_note-54]

After the crusade, the territory of present-day south Estonia and Latvia was named [Terra Mariana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terra_Mariana); later on it became known simply as [Livonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livonia).[^cite_note-55] Northern Estonia became the Danish [Duchy of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danish_Estonia), while the rest was divided between the Sword Brothers and [prince-bishoprics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince-bishopric) of [Dorpat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bishopric_of_Dorpat) and [Ösel–Wiek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bishopric_of_%C3%96sel%E2%80%93Wiek). In 1236, after suffering a [major defeat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Saule), the Sword Brothers merged into the [Teutonic Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teutonic_Order) becoming the [Livonian Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livonian_Order).[^cite_note-56] In the next decades there were several uprisings against the Teutonic rulers in Saaremaa. In 1343, a major [uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._George's_Night_Uprising) encompassed north Estonia and Saaremaa. The Teutonic Order suppressed the rebellion by 1345, and in 1346 the Danish king sold his possessions in Estonia to the Order.[^cite_note-57][^cite_note-58] The unsuccessful rebellion led to a consolidation of power for the upper-class German minority.[^cite_note-59] For the subsequent centuries [Low German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_German) remained the language of the ruling elite in both Estonian cities and the countryside.[^cite_note-60]



Lübeck Law Codex for Tallinn

[Reval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reval) ([Tallinn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallinn)), the capital of [Danish Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danish_Estonia) founded on the site of Lindanise, adopted the [Lübeck law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%BCbeck_law) and received full town rights in 1248.[^cite_note-61] The [Hanseatic League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanseatic_League) controlled trade on the Baltic Sea, and overall the four largest towns in Estonia became members: Reval, [Dorpat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dorpat) (Tartu), [Pernau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pernau) (Pärnu), and [Fellin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fellin) (Viljandi). Reval acted as a trade intermediary between [Novgorod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novgorod_Republic) and western Hanseatic cities, while Dorpat filled the same role with [Pskov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pskov_Land). Many artisans' and merchants [guilds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guilds) were formed during the period.[^cite_note-62] Protected by their stone walls and membership in the Hansa, prosperous cities like Reval and Dorpat often defied other rulers of the medieval [Livonian Confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livonian_Confederation).[^cite_note-63][^cite_note-65]

### Post-Reformation Era

Main articles: [Swedish Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish_Estonia), [Governorate of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governorate_of_Estonia), and [Governorate of Livonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governorate_of_Livonia)



*Academia Gustaviana* (now [University of Tartu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Tartu)) was founded in 1632 by King Gustaf II Adolf and was then the second university in the entire [Kingdom of Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Sweden).

The [Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformation) began in central Europe in 1517, and soon spread northward to Livonia despite some opposition by the Livonian Order.[^cite_note-66] Towns were the first to embrace Protestantism in the 1520s, and by the 1530s the majority of the landowners and rural population had adopted [Lutheranism](23371382.md).[^cite_note-Frucht121-67][^cite_note-68] Church services were now conducted in vernacular language, which initially meant [Low German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_German), but already from the 1530s onward the regular religious services were held in Estonian.[^cite_note-Frucht121-67][^cite_note-69]

During the 16th century, the expansionist monarchies of [Muscovy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Duchy_of_Moscow), Sweden, and [Poland–Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_union) consolidated power, posing a growing threat to decentralised Livonia weakened by disputes between cities, nobility, bishops, and the Order.[^cite_note-Frucht121-67][^cite_note-Hoover_Press-70]

In 1558, Tsar [Ivan the Terrible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_the_Terrible) of [Russia (Muscovy)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsardom_of_Russia) invaded Livonia, starting the [Livonian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livonian_War). The Livonian Order was decisively [defeated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Ergeme) in 1560. The majority of Livonia accepted Polish rule, while Reval and the nobles of northern Estonia swore loyalty to the Swedish king, and the Bishop of Ösel-Wiek sold his lands to the Danish king. Tsar Ivan's forces were at first able to conquer the larger part of Livonia, however in the 1570s the Polish-Lithuanian and Swedish armies went on the offensive and the war ended in 1583 with Russian defeat.[^cite_note-Hoover_Press-70][^cite_note-71] As a result of the war, northern Estonia became the Swedish [Duchy of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duchy_of_Estonia_\(1561%E2%80%931721\)), southern Estonia became the Polish [Duchy of Livonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duchy_of_Livonia), and Saaremaa remained under Danish control.[^cite_note-72]

In 1600, the [Polish–Swedish War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Swedish_War) broke out, causing further devastation. The protracted war ended in 1629 with Sweden [gaining Livonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish_Livonia), including the regions of Southern Estonia and Northern Latvia.[^cite_note-73] Danish Saaremaa was [transferred](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Treaty_of_Br%C3%B6msebro_\(1645\)) to Sweden in 1645.[^cite_note-74] The wars had halved the population of Estonia from about 250–270,000 people in the mid 16th century to 115–120,000 in the 1630s.[^cite_note-75] While large parts of the rural population remained in [serfdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serfdom) during the Swedish rule, legal reforms strengthened both serfs' and [free tenant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_tenant) farmers' land usage and inheritance rights – hence this period got the reputation of "The Good Old Swedish Time" in historical memory.[^cite_note-76] Swedish King [Gustaf II Adolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustavus_Adolphus_of_Sweden) established [gymnasiums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gymnasium_\(school\)) in Reval and Dorpat; the latter was upgraded to [Tartu University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tartu_University) in 1632. [Printing presses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Printing_presses) were also established in both towns. The beginnings of the Estonian public education system appeared in the 1680s, largely due to efforts of [Bengt Forselius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengt_Gottfried_Forselius), who also introduced orthographical reforms to written Estonian.[^cite_note-77] The population of Estonia grew rapidly until the [Great Famine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Famine_of_Estonia_\(1695%E2%80%931697\)) of 1695–97 in which 70,000–75,000 people died – about 20% of the population.[^cite_note-78]

During the 1700–1721 [Great Northern War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Northern_War), the [Tsardom of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsardom_of_Russia) (Muscovy) conquered the whole of Estonia by 1710.[^cite_note-79] The war again devastated the population of Estonia, with the 1712 population estimated at only 150,000–170,000.[^cite_note-80] In 1721, Estonia was divided into two [governorates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_governorates): the [Governorate of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governorate_of_Estonia), which included Tallinn and the northern part of Estonia, and the southern [Governorate of Livonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governorate_of_Livonia), which extended to the northern part of Latvia.[^cite_note-81] The tsarist administration restored all the political and landholding rights of the [local aristocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_German_nobility).[^cite_note-82] The rights of local farmers reached their lowest point, as serfdom completely dominated agricultural relations during the 18th century.[^cite_note-83] Serfdom was formally abolished in 1816–1819, but this initially had very little practical effect; major improvements in farmers' rights started with reforms in the mid-19th century.[^cite_note-84]

The reopening of the university in Tartu in 1802 gave opportunities for higher education to both Baltic German and a growing number of Estonian students. Among the latter were first public proponents of [Estonian nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_nationalism), such as young poet [Kristjan Jaak Peterson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kristjan_Jaak_Peterson). At the same time, the nationalist ideas of [Johann Gottfried Herder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Gottfried_Herder) greatly influenced the Baltic German intelligentsia to see the value in the native Estonian culture.[^cite_note-85] The resulting [Estophile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estophilia) movement gave rise to the [Learned Estonian Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Learned_Estonian_Society) and other scientific societies, supported Estonian-language education and founded the first newspapers in the Estonian language. They also began to value and collect [Estonian folklore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_folklore), including surviving pre-Christian myths and traditions.[^cite_note-86] Another sign of a rising Estonian national consciousness was a mass movement in South Estonia to convert to [Eastern Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy_in_Estonia) in the 1840s, following a famine and a promise for being rewarded with land.[^cite_note-87]

### National Awakening

Main article: [Estonian national awakening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_national_awakening)



[Friedrich Reinhold Kreutzwald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Reinhold_Kreutzwald), author of the Estonian national epic *Kalevipoeg*, (by Johann Köler, 1864)

By the 1850s, several leading figures were promoting an Estonian national identity among the general populace. Widespread farm buyouts by Estonians and the resulting rapidly growing class of land-owning farmers provided the economic basis for the political affirmation of the Estonian identity. In 1857, [Johann Voldemar Jannsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Voldemar_Jannsen) started publishing one of the first successful circulating Estonian-language weekly newspapers, *[Perno Postimees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C3%A4rnu_Postimees)*, and began popularising the denomination of oneself as *eestlane* (Estonian).[^cite_note-EE286-88] Schoolmaster [Carl Robert Jakobson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Robert_Jakobson) and clergyman [Jakob Hurt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jakob_Hurt) became leading figures in a [nationalist movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_nationalism), encouraging Estonian farmers to take pride in their language and ethnic Estonian identity.[^cite_note-89]

The first nationwide movements formed in the 1860s, such as a campaign to establish the Estonian language Alexander School, the founding of the [Society of Estonian Literati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society_of_Estonian_Literati) and the [Estonian Students' Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Students'_Society), and the first [national song festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Song_Festival), held in 1869 in Tartu.[^cite_note-Raun59-90][^cite_note-EE287-91][^cite_note-92] Linguistic reforms helped to develop the Estonian language.[^cite_note-93] The national [epic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epic_poetry) *[Kalevipoeg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalevipoeg)* was published in 1857, and 1870 saw the first performances of [Estonian theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatre_of_Estonia).[^cite_note-94][^cite_note-CoE-95] In 1878 a major split happened in the national movement. The moderate wing led by Hurt focused on development of culture and Estonian education, while the radical wing led by Jakobson started demanding increased political and economical rights.[^cite_note-EE287-91]

In the 1890s, the central government of the Russian Empire launched a [Russification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russification) campaign in order to tie the Baltic governorates more closely to the empire administratively and culturally.[^cite_note-Raun59-90] Russian language replaced German and Estonian in most secondary schools and universities, and many societal activities in local languages were suppressed.[^cite_note-CoE-95] In the late 1890s, prominent figures like [Jaan Tõnisson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaan_T%C3%B5nisson) and [Konstantin Päts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konstantin_P%C3%A4ts) rose in a new surge of nationalism. By the early 20th century, Estonians started taking over their German-run town governments.[^cite_note-EE291-96] During the [1905 Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1905_Russian_Revolution), the first legal Estonian political parties were founded. An Estonian national congress was convened and demanded the unification of Estonian areas into a single autonomous territory and an end to Russification. The unrest was accompanied by both peaceful [demonstrations](6787314.md) and violent [riots](146704.md). The Tsarist government responded with a brutal crackdown; some 500 people were executed and hundreds more jailed or deported to Siberia.[^cite_note-97][^cite_note-EE292-98]

In 1917, following the [collapse of the Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/February_Revolution), the [Russian Provisional Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Provisional_Government) yielded to popular demand and the two main Estonian-speaking governorates were merged into one [Autonomous Governorate of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Governorate_of_Estonia), and the [Estonian Provincial Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Provincial_Assembly) was then elected.[^cite_note-99] [Bolsheviks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsheviks) [seized power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolshevik_coup) in Estonia in November 1917, and disbanded the Provincial Assembly. However, the Provincial Assembly established the [Salvation Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Salvation_Committee), and during the short interlude between Russian retreat and [German arrival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Faustschlag), the committee [declared the independence of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Declaration_of_Independence) on 24 February 1918, and formed the [Estonian Provisional Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Provisional_Government). [German occupation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_occupation_of_Estonia_during_World_War_I) immediately followed, but after Germany's capitulation in World War I, they were forced to return power to the Estonian government on 19 November 1918.[^cite_note-100][^cite_note-101]



Estonian armoured train during the war of independence

On 28 November 1918 [Soviet Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Russia) invaded, starting the [Estonian War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_War_of_Independence).[^cite_note-102] The [Red Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Army) came within 30 km of Tallinn, but in January 1919, the [Estonian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Army), led by [Johan Laidoner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johan_Laidoner), went on a counter-offensive, ejecting Bolshevik forces from Estonia within a few months. Renewed Soviet attacks failed, and in spring, the Estonian army, in co-operation with [White Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_movement) forces, advanced into Russia and [Latvia](17514.md).[^cite_note-Pinder76-103][^cite_note-104] In June 1919, Estonia [defeated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_C%C4%93sis_\(1919\)) the [German Landeswehr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltische_Landeswehr) which had attempted to dominate Latvia. The Red Army launched a [final offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Krivasoo) against [Narva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narva) in late 1919, but failed to achieve a breakthrough. On 2 February 1920, the [Tartu Peace Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Tartu_\(Russian%E2%80%93Estonian\)) was signed by Estonia and Soviet Russia, with the latter pledging to permanently give up all sovereign claims to Estonia.[^cite_note-Pinder76-103][^cite_note-105]

In April 1919, the [Estonian Constituent Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Constituent_Assembly) was elected. They passed a sweeping [land reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Land_Reform_of_1919) and adopted a highly [liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism) [constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Estonia), establishing Estonia as a [parliamentary](794927.md) democracy.[^cite_note-106][^cite_note-107] Estonia joined the [League of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Nations) in 1921.[^cite_note-Ginneken-108] Estonia enjoyed rapid economic growth during the interwar period. Land reforms improved the farmers' conditions, but the country also prospered from industrialisation and the development of oil shale mining. With independence, most economic links with Russia dissolved, but trade rapidly reoriented towards Western markets.[^cite_note-109] Estonia's cultural-autonomy law for ethnic minorities, adopted in 1925, was one of the most liberal of its time.[^cite_note-110]

The [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression) strained Estonia's political system, and in 1933 the right-wing [Vaps movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaps_movement) spearheaded a constitutional reform establishing a strong presidency.[^cite_note-111][^cite_note-112] On 12 March 1934 the acting head of state, [Konstantin Päts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konstantin_P%C3%A4ts), extended a state of emergency over the entire country, under the pretext that the Vaps movement had been planning a coup. Päts ruled by decree for several years while parliament did not reconvene ("[era of silence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Era_of_silence)").[^cite_note-113] The Päts régime was relatively benign compared to other authoritarian régimes in [interwar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interwar_period) Europe, never using violence against political opponents.[^cite_note-114] A 1937 referendum adopted a new constitution. In 1938, an election including opposition candidates provided [a new bicameral parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1938_Estonian_parliamentary_election).[^cite_note-115] Estonia signed non-aggression pacts [with the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Estonian_Non-Aggression_Pact) in 1932, and [with Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German%E2%80%93Estonian_Non-Aggression_Pact) in 1939.[^cite_note-116] In 1939, Estonia declared neutrality, but this proved futile in [World War II](32927.md).[^cite_note-117]

### Independence

Main articles: [Estonian Declaration of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Declaration_of_Independence), [Estonian War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_War_of_Independence), and [History of Estonia § Interwar period (1920–1939)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Estonia#Interwar_period_\(1920%E2%80%931939\))



Estonian Constituent Assembly, opening session on 23 April 1919

In 1917, after the [February Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/February_Revolution), the governorate of Estonia [was expanded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Governorate_of_Estonia) by the [Russian Provisional Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Provisional_Government) to include Estonian-speaking areas of Livonia and was granted autonomy, enabling the election of the [Estonian Provincial Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Provincial_Assembly).[^cite_note-118] The [Bolsheviks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsheviks) [seized power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolshevik_coup) in Estonia in November 1917, and the Provincial Assembly was disbanded. However, the Provincial Assembly established the [Salvation Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Salvation_Committee), and during the short interlude between Bolshevik retreat and [German arrival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Faustschlag), the committee [declared independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Declaration_of_Independence) and formed the [Estonian Provisional Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Provisional_Government) on 24 February 1918 in the capital Tallinn. [German occupation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_occupation_of_Estonia_during_World_War_I) immediately followed, but after their defeat in World War I, the Germans were forced to hand over power back to the Provisional Government of independent Estonia on 19 November 1918.[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120]

On 28 November 1918, [Soviet Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Russia) invaded, starting the [Estonian War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_War_of_Independence).[^cite_note-121] The [Red Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Army) came within 30 km of Tallinn, but in January 1919, the [Estonian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Army), led by [Johan Laidoner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johan_Laidoner), went on a counter-offensive, ejecting Bolshevik forces from Estonia within a few weeks. Renewed Soviet attacks failed, and in the spring of 1919, the Estonian army, in co-operation with [White Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_movement) forces, advanced into Russia and [Latvia](17514.md).[^cite_note-Pinder76-103][^cite_note-122] In June 1919, Estonia [defeated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_C%C4%93sis_\(1919\)) the [German Landeswehr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltische_Landeswehr) which had attempted to dominate Latvia, restoring power to the government of [Kārlis Ulmanis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C4%81rlis_Ulmanis) there. After the collapse of the White Russian forces, the Red Army launched a [major offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Krivasoo) against [Narva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narva) in late 1919, but failed to achieve a breakthrough. On 2 February 1920, the [Tartu Peace Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Tartu_\(Russian%E2%80%93Estonian\)) was signed by Estonia and Soviet Russia, with the latter pledging to permanently give up all sovereign claims to Estonia.[^cite_note-Pinder76-103][^cite_note-123]

In April 1919, the [Estonian Constituent Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Constituent_Assembly) was elected. The Constituent Assembly passed a sweeping [land reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Land_Reform_of_1919) expropriating large estates, and adopted a new highly [liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism) [constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Estonia) establishing Estonia as a [parliamentary](794927.md) democracy.[^cite_note-124][^cite_note-125] In 1924, the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) organised a communist [coup attempt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1924_Estonian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat_attempt), which quickly failed.[^cite_note-126] Estonia's cultural-autonomy law for ethnic minorities, adopted in 1925, is widely recognised as one of the most liberal in the world at that time.[^cite_note-127] The [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression) put heavy pressure on Estonia's political system, and in 1933, the right-wing [Vaps movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaps_movement) spearheaded a constitutional reform establishing a strong presidency.[^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129] On 12 March 1934 the acting head of state, [Konstantin Päts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konstantin_P%C3%A4ts), extended a state of emergency over the entire country, under the pretext that the Vaps movement had been planning a coup. Päts went on to rule by decree for several years, while the parliament did not reconvene ("[era of silence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Era_of_silence)").[^cite_note-130] A new constitution was adopted in a 1937 referendum, and in 1938 a new bicameral [parliament was elected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1938_Estonian_parliamentary_election) in a popular vote, where both pro-government and opposition candidates participated.[^cite_note-131] The Päts régime was relatively benign compared to other authoritarian régimes in [interwar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interwar_period) Europe, and the régime never used violence against political opponents.[^cite_note-132]

Estonia joined the [League of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Nations) in 1921.[^cite_note-Ginneken-108] Attempts to establish a [larger alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Accord) together with [Finland](10577.md), [Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Polish_Republic), and Latvia failed, with only a mutual-defence pact being signed with Latvia in 1923, and later was followed up with the [Baltic Entente](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_Entente) of 1934.[^cite_note-133][^cite_note-134] In the 1930s, Estonia also engaged in secret [military co-operation with Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish%E2%80%93Estonian_defence_cooperation).[^cite_note-135] Non-aggression pacts were signed [with the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Estonian_Non-Aggression_Pact) in 1932, and [with Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German%E2%80%93Estonian_Non-Aggression_Pact) in 1939.[^cite_note-Ginneken-108][^cite_note-136] In 1939, Estonia declared neutrality, but this proved futile in [World War II](32927.md).[^cite_note-137]

### World War II

Main articles: [Estonia in World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonia_in_World_War_II) and [Occupation of the Baltic states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_the_Baltic_states)



The [Red Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Army) troops moving into military bases in Estonia in October 1939, after the Soviet Union had forced Estonia to sign the [Bases Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Estonian_Mutual_Assistance_Treaty).

A week before the outbreak of [World War II](32927.md), on 23 August 1939, [Nazi Germany](21212.md) and the [Stalinist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stalinism) Soviet Union signed the [Molotov–Ribbentrop Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molotov%E2%80%93Ribbentrop_Pact). In the pact's [secret protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molotov%E2%80%93Ribbentrop_Pact_negotiations) Poland, Romania, Lithuania, Latvia, Estonia and Finland were divided between USSR and Germany into "spheres of influence", with Estonia assigned to the Soviet "sphere".[^cite_note-138] On 24 September 1939, the Soviet dictator [Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stalin) presented the Estonian government an ultimatum demanding that Estonia immediately sign a treaty that would allow the USSR to establish military bases in Estonia, or else face war. The Estonian government decided to avoid military conflict, and a "[mutual assistance treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Estonian_Mutual_Assistance_Treaty)" was signed in Moscow on 28 September 1939.[^cite_note-139] On 14 June 1940 the Soviet Union instituted a full naval and air blockade on Estonia. On the same day, the airliner *[Kaleva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaleva_\(airplane\))* was shot down by the [Soviet Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Air_Force). On 16 June, the USSR presented an ultimatum demanding completely free passage of the Red Army into Estonia and the establishment of a pro-Soviet government. Feeling that resistance was hopeless, the Estonian government complied and, on the next day, the whole country was occupied.[^cite_note-140][^cite_note-141] On 6 August 1940, Estonia was annexed by the Soviet Union as the [Estonian SSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_SSR).[^cite_note-malksoo-142]



The capital Tallinn after [bombing by the Soviet Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombing_of_Tallinn_in_World_War_II) during the war on the [Eastern Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Front_\(World_War_II\)) in March 1944

The USSR established a repressive wartime regime in occupied Estonia. Many of the country's high-ranking civil and military officials, intelligentsia and industrialists were arrested. Soviet repressions culminated on 14 June 1941 with [mass deportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/June_deportation) of around 11,000 people to [Russia](25391.md).[^cite_note-Miljan110-143][^cite_note-144] When [Operation Barbarossa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Barbarossa) (accompanied by Estonian [guerrilla soldiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guerilla_warfare) called "[Forest Brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_Brothers)"[^cite_note-145]) began against the Soviet Union on 22 June 1941 in the form of the "[Summer War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Summer_War)" ([Estonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_language): *Suvesõda*), around 34,000 young Estonian men were forcibly drafted into the [Red Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Army), fewer than 30% of whom survived the war. Soviet [destruction battalions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Destruction_battalions) initiated a scorched earth policy. Political prisoners who could not be evacuated [were executed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NKVD_prisoner_massacres) by the [NKVD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NKVD).[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147] Many Estonians went into the forest, starting an anti-Soviet guerrilla campaign. In July, German [Wehrmacht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wehrmacht) reached south Estonia. The USSR [evacuated Tallinn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_evacuation_of_Tallinn) in late August with massive losses, and capture of the Estonian islands was completed by German forces in October.[^cite_note-pinder80-148]

Initially, many Estonians hoped that Germany would help to restore Estonia's independence, but this soon proved to be in vain. Only a puppet [collaborationist administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Self-Administration) was established, and [occupied Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_occupation_of_Estonia_during_World_War_II) was merged into [Reichskommissariat Ostland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reichskommissariat_Ostland), with its economy being fully subjugated to German military needs.[^cite_note-149] About a thousand [Estonian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Jews) who had not managed to leave were almost all [quickly killed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_in_Estonia) in 1941. Numerous forced labour camps were established where thousands of Estonians, foreign Jews, [Romani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_people), and [Soviet prisoners of war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_mistreatment_of_Soviet_prisoners_of_war) perished.[^cite_note-CommissionReport-150] German occupation authorities started recruiting men into small [volunteer units](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Legion) but, as these efforts provided meagre results and the military situation worsened, forced conscription was instituted in 1943, eventually leading to formation of the [Estonian Waffen-SS division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20th_Waffen_Grenadier_Division_of_the_SS_\(1st_Estonian\)).[^cite_note-151] Thousands of Estonians who did not want to fight in the [German military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wehrmacht) escaped to Finland, where [many volunteered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Infantry_Regiment_200) to fight together with Finns [against Soviets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continuation_War).[^cite_note-152]

The Red Army reached the Estonian borders again in early 1944, but its advance into Estonia was stopped in [heavy fighting near Narva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Narva_\(1944\)) for six months by German forces, including numerous Estonian units.[^cite_note-Raun159-153] In March, the Soviet Air Force carried out heavy bombing raids [against Tallinn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombing_of_Tallinn_in_World_War_II) and other Estonian towns.[^cite_note-154] In July, the Soviets started [a major offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallinn_Offensive) from the south, forcing the Germans to abandon mainland Estonia in September and the Estonian islands in November.[^cite_note-Raun159-153] As German forces were retreating from Tallinn, the last pre-war prime minister [Jüri Uluots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%BCri_Uluots) [appointed a government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Committee_of_the_Republic_of_Estonia) headed by [Otto Tief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_Tief) in an unsuccessful attempt to restore Estonia's independence.[^cite_note-155] Tens of thousands of people, including most of the [Estonian Swedes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Swedes), fled westwards to avoid the new Soviet occupation.[^cite_note-156]

Overall, Estonia lost about 25% of its population through deaths, deportations and evacuations in World War II.[^cite_note-157] Estonia also suffered some irrevocable territorial losses, as the Soviet Union transferred border areas comprising about 5% of Estonian pre-war territory from the Estonian SSR to the [Russian SFSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_SFSR).[^cite_note-158]

### Second Soviet occupation

Main articles: [Estonian Soviet Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic), [Occupation of the Baltic states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_the_Baltic_states), and [Baltic states under Soviet rule (1944–1991)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_states_under_Soviet_rule_\(1944%E2%80%931991\))



[Estonian partisans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_partisans), the "Forest Brothers".

Thousands of Estonians opposing the second Soviet occupation joined a guerrilla movement known as the "[Forest Brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_partisans)". The armed resistance was heaviest in the first few years after the war, but Soviet authorities gradually wore it down through attrition, and resistance effectively ceased to exist in the mid-1950s.[^cite_note-159] The Soviets initiated a [policy of collectivisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collectivization_in_the_Soviet_Union), but as farmers remained opposed to it a campaign of terror was unleashed. In March 1949 about 20,000 Estonians [were deported](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Priboi) to Siberia. Collectivization was fully completed soon afterwards.[^cite_note-Miljan110-143][^cite_note-160]

The Russian-dominated occupation authorities under the Soviet Union began [Russification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russification), with hundreds of thousands of ethnic [Russians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russians_in_Estonia) and other "[Soviet people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_people)" being induced to settle in occupied Estonia, in a process which eventually threatened to turn indigenous Estonians into a minority in their own native land.[^cite_note-Taagepera97-161] In 1945 Estonians formed 97% of the population, but by 1989 their share of the population had fallen to 62%.[^cite_note-162] Occupying authorities carried out campaigns of ethnic cleansing, mass deportation of indigenous populations, and mass colonization by Russian settlers which led to Estonia losing 3% of its native population.[^cite_note-163] By March 1949, 60,000 people were deported from Estonia and 50,000 from Latvia to the [gulag system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulag) in Siberia, where death rates were 30%. The only permitted party was the ruling [Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Estonia), along with its youth organization.[^cite_note-164] Economically, heavy industry was strongly prioritised, but this did not improve the well-being of the local population, and caused massive environmental damage through pollution.[^cite_note-165] Living standards under the Soviet occupation kept falling further behind nearby independent Finland.[^cite_note-Taagepera97-161] The country was heavily militarised, with closed military areas covering 2% of territory.[^cite_note-166] Islands and most of the coastal areas were turned into a restricted border zone which required a special permit for entry.[^cite_note-167] Estonia was quite closed until the second half of the 1960s, when gradually Estonians began to covertly watch [Finnish television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_television) in the northern parts of the country, thus getting a better picture of the way of life behind the [Iron Curtain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Curtain).[^cite_note-168]

The majority of Western countries [considered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stimson_Doctrine) the annexation of Estonia by the Soviet Union illegal.[^cite_note-169] [Legal continuity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_continuity_of_the_Baltic_states) of the Estonian state was preserved through the [government-in-exile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_government-in-exile) and the Estonian [diplomatic representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Diplomatic_Service_\(1940%E2%80%9391\)) which Western governments continued to recognise.[^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171]

### Independence restored



In the [Baltic Way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_Way) on 23 August 1989, two million people formed a human chain across three countries in a mass demonstration against the Soviet occupation.

The introduction of *[perestroika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perestroika)* by the Soviet central authorities in 1985 made open political activity possible again in Estonia a few years later, which triggered an independence restoration process later known as *laulev revolutsioon* ("[the Singing revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singing_Revolution)").[^cite_note-172] The environmental *Fosforiidisõda* ("[Phosphorite war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phosphorite_War)") campaign became the first major protest movement against the [central government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_the_Soviet_Union).[^cite_note-173] In 1988, new political movements appeared, such as the [Popular Front of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_Front_of_Estonia), which came to represent the moderate wing in the independence movement, and the more radical [Estonian National Independence Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_National_Independence_Party), which was the first non-communist party in the Soviet Union and demanded full restoration of independence.[^cite_note-174] On 16 November 1988, after the first non-rigged multi-candidate elections in half a century, the parliament of Soviet-controlled Estonia issued the [Sovereignty Declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Sovereignty_Declaration), asserting the primacy of Estonian laws. Over the next two years, many other administrative parts (or ["republics"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republics_of_the_Soviet_Union)) of the USSR followed the Estonian example, issuing similar declarations.[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176] On 23 August 1989, about 2 million Estonians, Latvians and Lithuanians participated in a mass demonstration, forming the [Baltic Way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_Way) human chain across the three countries.[^cite_note-177] In February 1990, elections were held to form the [Congress of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Estonia).[^cite_note-178] In March 1991, a [referendum was held](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Estonian_independence_referendum) where 78.4% of voters supported full independence. During the [coup attempt in Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Soviet_coup_d'%C3%A9tat_attempt), Estonia [declared restoration of independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_restoration_of_Independence) on 20 August 1991.[^cite_note-179]

Soviet authorities recognised Estonian independence on 6 September 1991, and on 17 September Estonia was admitted into the [United Nations](31769.md).[^cite_note-Dillon164-180] The last units of the [Russian army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_army) left Estonia in 1994.[^cite_note-181]

In 1992 radical economic reforms were launched for switching over to a market economy, including privatisation and currency reform.[^cite_note-182] Estonia has been a member of the [WTO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization) since 13 November 1999.[^cite_note-183]

Since regaining independence in 1991, Estonian foreign policy has been aligned with other [Western democracies](21208200.md), and in 2004 Estonia joined both the [European Union](9317.md) and [NATO](21133.md).[^cite_note-184] On 9 December 2010, Estonia became a member of [OECD](33853117.md).[^cite_note-185] On 1 January 2011, Estonia joined the [eurozone](184391.md) and adopted the [euro](9472.md), the single currency of EU.[^cite_note-186] Estonia was a member of the [UN Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Security_council) from 2020 to 2021.[^cite_note-187]

The [100th Anniversary of the Estonian Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/100th_Anniversary_of_the_Estonian_Republic) was celebrated on 24 February 2018.[^cite_note-188]

[English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language) is the most widely spoken foreign language in Estonia today. According to the most recent (2021) census data 76% of the population can speak a foreign language. After English, Russian is the second most widely spoken foreign language in Estonia, and in the census 17% of the native speakers of standard Estonian reported that they can also speak a dialect of Estonian.[^cite_note-189][^cite_note-PopulationByNationality-190]

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Estonia)



The cliff at Valaste Falls illustrates the stratigraphy of various geological eras

Estonia is situated in [Europe](9239.md),[^cite_note-location-16], on the [East European Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_European_Plain)[^cite_note-Raukas_2018-191][^cite_note-EEA-192][^cite_note-193][^cite_note-194] Estonian territory covers 45,335 km<sup>2</sup> (17,504 sq mi), of which internal waters comprise 4.6%.[^cite_note-Raukas_2018-191] When including the [territorial sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_sea), the Estonian border encompasses 70,177 km<sup>2</sup> (27,095 sq mi).[^cite_note-195]

Estonia coastline is indented stretching for 3,794 kilometres (2,357 mi)including the islands, and is notable for its [limestone cliffs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cliffed_coast) at the northern coast and largest islands.[^cite_note-EEA-192][^cite_note-196] The total number of [Estonian islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_islands_of_Estonia), including those in internal waters, is 2,355, of which 2,222 are in the Baltic Sea. The largest islands are Saaremaa and Hiiumaa. There are over 1560 [natural lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_lakes_of_Estonia), the largest being Lake Peipus at the border of Russia, and [Võrtsjärv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%B5rtsj%C3%A4rv) in central Estonia. Additionally there are many artificial water reservoirs. There are over [7000 rivers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_rivers_of_Estonia), streams, and canals in the country; of these, only ten are longer than 100 kilometres (62 mi). In terms of discharge, [Narva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narva_\(river\)) is the largest of the rivers. The longest rivers of Estonia are [Võhandu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%B5handu) — 162 kilometres (101 mi) and [Pärnu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C3%A4rnu_\(river\)) —144 kilometres (89 mi), followed by the [Põltsamaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C3%B5ltsamaa_\(river\)), [Pedja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedja_\(river\)), [Kasari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kasari_\(river\)), [Keila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keila_\(river\)), and [Jägala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%A4gala_\(river\)) rivers. [Bogs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bogs) and [mires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mire) cover 23.2% of the land. Generally the terrain is flat, average elevation above the sea level being about 50 metres (164 ft). Only 10% of the country's terrain is greater than 100 metres (328 ft) in height, with [Haanja Upland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haanja_Upland) containing the highest peak, [Suur Munamägi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suur_Munam%C3%A4gi), at 318 metres (1,043 ft).[^cite_note-Raukas_2018-191]

### Climate

Main article: [Climate of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Estonia)

Estonia is situated in the [temperate climate zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate), and in the transition zone between [maritime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maritime_climate) and [continental climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification#Group_D:_Continental/microthermal_climates), characterized by warm summers and fairly mild winters. Estonia's climate is notably milder than that of other regions at the same latitude due to the moderating effects of the [North Atlantic Current](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Atlantic_Current). The prevailing weather patterns in Estonia are influenced by active cyclonic activity in the northern [Atlantic Ocean](698.md). This results in strong winds, precipitation, and abrupt temperature fluctuations, especially during the autumn and winter months. The westerly winds carry moist maritime air far into continental interior, leading to milder temperatures in winter and cooler conditions in summer compared to the continental areas further away from the coast. Coastal regions and islands generally enjoy a milder climate, as the Baltic Sea moderates temperatures, keeping coastal areas warmer in winter and cooler in summer.[^cite_note-estonica_climate-197][^cite_note-Raukas_2018-191][^cite_note-EEA-192] The calculated mean temperature for the country's territory ranges from 17.8 °C (64.0 °F) in July to −3.8 °C (25.2 °F) in February, with the annual average being 6.4 °C (43.5 °F).[^cite_note-198] The highest recorded temperature is 35.6 °C (96.1 °F) from 1992, although the unofficial record high temperature is 38.0 °C (104.8 °F) from the same year[^cite_note-199] and the lowest is −43.5 °C (−46.3 °F) from 1940.[^cite_note-200] The annual average precipitation is 662 millimetres (26.1 in),[^cite_note-201] with the daily record being 148 millimetres (5.8 in).[^cite_note-202] Snow cover varies significantly on different years. Prevailing winds are westerly, southwesterly, and southerly, the average annual wind speed varies between 2.1 m/s and 6.1 m/s, being smaller inland and the largest on the west coast. As a calculated average, Estonia receives 1830 hours of sunshine per year.[^cite_note-203]

### Biodiversity

Main articles: [Fauna of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauna_of_Estonia) and [Protected areas of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protected_areas_of_Estonia)



Scots pine *[Pinus sylvestris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinus_sylvestris)* is the most common tree species in Estonian forests.

Due to varied climatic and soil conditions, and plethora of sea and internal waters, Estonia is one of the most biodiverse regions among the similar sized territories at the same [latitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latitude).[^cite_note-EEA-192] Many species extinct in most other European countries can be still found in Estonia.[^cite_note-Taylor_1-204]

Recorded species include 64 [mammals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mammal), 11 [amphibians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amphibian), and 5 [reptiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reptile).[^cite_note-Raukas_2018-191] Large mammals present in Estonia include the [grey wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_wolf), [lynx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_lynx), [brown bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_bear), [red fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_fox), [badger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_badger), [wild boar](52255.md), [moose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moose), [roe deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roe_deer), [beaver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_beaver), [otter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_otter), [grey seal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grey_seal), and [ringed seal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ringed_seal). The critically endangered [European mink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_mink) has been successfully reintroduced to the island of Hiiumaa, and the rare [Siberian flying squirrel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siberian_flying_squirrel) is present in east Estonia.[^cite_note-Taylor_1-204] The [red deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_deer), once extirpated, has also been successfully reintroduced.[^cite_note-205] In the beginning of the 21st century, an isolated population of [European jackals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_jackal) was confirmed in Western Estonia, much further north than their earlier known range. The number of jackals has grown quickly in coastal areas of Estonia and can be found in [Matsalu National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matsalu_National_Park).[^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207] Introduced mammals include [sika deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sika_deer), [fallow deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_fallow_deer), [raccoon dog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_raccoon_dog), [muskrat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muskrat), and [American mink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_mink).[^cite_note-Raukas_2018-191]

Over 300 bird species have been found in Estonia, including the [white-tailed eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White-tailed_eagle), [lesser spotted eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesser_spotted_eagle), [golden eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_eagle), [western capercaillie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_capercaillie), [black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Stork) and [white stork](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_stork), numerous species of [owls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Owls), [waders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wader), [geese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geese) and many others.[^cite_note-Taylor_2-208] The [barn swallow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barn_swallow) is the [national bird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_bird) of Estonia.[^cite_note-209]



Estonia is one of the few countries where critically endangered [European mink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_mink) is present in the wild

[Phytogeographically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phytogeography), Estonia is shared between the Central European and Eastern European provinces of the [Circumboreal Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circumboreal_Region) within the [Boreal Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boreal_Kingdom). According to the [WWF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Wide_Fund_for_Nature), the territory of Estonia belongs to the [ecoregion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecoregion) of [Sarmatic mixed forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarmatic_mixed_forests).[^cite_note-DinersteinOlson2017-210] Estonia has a rich composition of floristic groups, with estimated 6000 (3461 identified) [fungi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fungi), 3000 (2500 identified) [algae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algae) and [cyanobacteria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyanobacteria), 850 (786 identified) [lichens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lichens), and 600 (507 identified) [bryophytes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bryophytes). Forests cover approximately half of the country. 87 native and over 500 introduced tree and bush species have been identified, with most prevalent tree species being [pine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pine) (41%), [birch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birch) (28%), and [spruce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spruce) (23%).[^cite_note-Raukas_2018-191] Since 1969, the [cornflower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centaurea_cyanus) (*Centaurea cyanus*) has been the national flower of Estonia.[^cite_note-211]

[Protected areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protected_areas_of_Estonia) cover 19.4% of Estonian land and 23% of its total area together with territorial sea. Overall there are 3,883 protected natural objects, including 6 national parks, 231 nature conservation areas, and 154 landscape reserves.[^cite_note-212]

## Politics

Main articles: [Politics of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Estonia), [List of political parties in Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_political_parties_in_Estonia), and [Elections in Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Estonia)



[Alar Karis](67085807.md)\
[President](1581712.md) since 2021



[Kristen Michal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kristen_Michal)\
[Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Estonia) since 2024



The seat of the Parliament of Estonia in Toompea Castle

Estonia is a unitary [parliamentary republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_republic). The unicameral parliament [Riigikogu](794927.md) serves as the legislature and [the government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Estonia) as the executive.[^cite_note-Toots-213]

Estonian parliament Riigikogu is elected by citizens over 18 years of age for a four-year term by [proportional representation](23298.md), and has 101 members. Riigikogu's responsibilities include approval and preservation of the national government, passing legal acts, passing the state budget, and conducting parliamentary supervision. On proposal of [the president](1581712.md) Riigikogu appoints the Chief Justice of the Supreme Court, the chairman of the board of the Bank of Estonia, the Auditor General, the Legal Chancellor, and the Commander-in-Chief of the Defence Forces.[^cite_note-214][^cite_note-215]

The [Government of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Estonia) is formed by the [Prime Minister of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Estonia) at recommendation of the President, and approved by the Riigikogu. The government, headed by the Prime Minister, carries out domestic and foreign policy. Ministers head ministries and represent its interests in the government. Sometimes ministers with no associated ministry are appointed, known as *ministers without portfolio*.[^cite_note-216] Estonia has been ruled by coalition governments because no party has been able to obtain an absolute majority in the parliament.[^cite_note-Toots-213]

The head of the state is [the President](1581712.md) who has a primarily representative and ceremonial role. There is no [popular vote](652563.md) on the election of the president, but the president is elected by the Riigikogu, or by a special electoral college.[^cite_note-217] The President proclaims the laws passed in the Riigikogu, and has the right to refuse proclamation and return law in question for a new debate and decision. If Riigikogu passes the law unamended, then the President has right to propose to the Supreme Court to declare the law unconstitutional. The President also represents the country in international relations.[^cite_note-Toots-213][^cite_note-218]

The [Constitution of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Estonia) also provides possibility for direct democracy through referendum, although since adoption of the constitution in 1992 the only referendum has been the [referendum on European Union membership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_Estonian_European_Union_membership_referendum) in 2003.[^cite_note-219]

Estonia has pursued the development of the [e-government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E-government), with 99 percent of the public services being available on the web 24 hours a day.[^cite_note-220] In 2005, [Estonia became](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_voting_in_Estonia) the first country in the world to introduce nationwide binding Internet voting in local elections of 2005.[^cite_note-221] In the [2023 parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Estonian_parliamentary_election) 51% of the total votes were cast over the internet, becoming the first time when more than half of votes were cast online.[^cite_note-222]

In the most recent [parliamentary elections of 2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Estonian_parliamentary_election), six parties gained seats at Riigikogu. The head of the [Reform Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Reform_Party), [Kaja Kallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaja_Kallas), [formed the government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaja_Kallas'_third_cabinet) together with [Estonia 200](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonia_200) and [Social Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Democratic_Party_\(Estonia\)), while [Conservative People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_People's_Party_of_Estonia), [Centre Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Centre_Party) and [Isamaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isamaa) became the opposition.[^cite_note-223][^cite_note-224]

### Law

Main articles: [Law of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Estonia) and [Constitution of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Estonia)

See also: [LGBT rights in Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Estonia)

The [Constitution of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Estonia) is the fundamental law, establishing the constitutional order based on five principles: human dignity, democracy, rule of law, social state, and the Estonian identity.[^cite_note-225] Estonia has a [civil law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_law_\(legal_system\)) legal system based on the Germanic legal model.[^cite_note-226] The court system has a three-level structure. The first instance are county courts which handle all criminal and civil cases, and administrative courts which hear complaints about government and local officials, and other public disputes. The [second instance](643.md) are district courts which handle appeals about the first instance decisions.[^cite_note-227] The [Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Estonia) is the [court of cassation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_of_cassation), conducts constitutional review, and has 19 members.[^cite_note-228] The judiciary is independent, judges are appointed for life, and can be removed from office only when convicted of a crime.[^cite_note-229] The justice system has been rated among the most efficient in the European Union by the EU Justice Scoreboard.[^cite_note-230] As of June 2023, gay registered partners and married couples have the right to adopt. [Gay couples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Estonia) gained the right to marriage in Estonia in 2024.[^cite_note-231][^cite_note-232]

### Foreign relations

Main articles: [Foreign relations of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Estonia) and [Diplomatic missions of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diplomatic_missions_of_Estonia)

Estonia was a member of the [League of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Nations) from 22 September 1921, and became a member of the [United Nations](31769.md) on 17 September 1991.[^cite_note-233][^cite_note-DFAT-234] Since restoration of independence Estonia has pursued close relations with the [Western countries](21208200.md), and has been member of [NATO](21133.md) and the [European Union](9317.md) since 2004.[^cite_note-DFAT-234] In 2007, Estonia joined the [Schengen Area](16565139.md), and in 2011 the [Eurozone](184391.md).[^cite_note-DFAT-234] The [European Union Agency for large-scale IT systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eu-Lisa) is based in Tallinn, and started operations at the end of 2012.[^cite_note-235] Estonia held the [Presidency of the Council of the European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_the_Council_of_the_European_Union) in the second half of 2017.[^cite_note-236]

Since the early 1990s, Estonia has been involved in active trilateral [Baltic states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_states) co-operation with Latvia and Lithuania, and Nordic-Baltic co-operation with the [Nordic countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_countries). Estonia is a member of the interparliamentary [Baltic Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_Assembly), the intergovernmental [Baltic Council of Ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_Council_of_Ministers) and the [Council of the Baltic Sea States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_the_Baltic_Sea_States).[^cite_note-237] Estonia has built close relationship with the Nordic countries, especially [Finland](10577.md) and [Sweden](5058739.md), and is a member of [Nordic-Baltic Eight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic-Baltic_Eight).[^cite_note-DFAT-234][^cite_note-NB8-238] Joint Nordic-Baltic projects include the education programme Nordplus[^cite_note-239] and mobility programmes for business and industry[^cite_note-240] and for public administration.[^cite_note-241] The [Nordic Council of Ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_Council_of_Ministers) has an office in [Tallinn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallinn) with a subsidiaries in [Tartu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tartu) and [Narva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narva).[^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243] The Baltic states are members of [Nordic Investment Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_Investment_Bank), European Union's [Nordic Battle Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_Battle_Group), and in 2011 were invited to co-operate with [Nordic Defence Cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_Defence_Cooperation) in selected activities.[^cite_note-244][^cite_note-245][^cite_note-246][^cite_note-247]

The beginning of the attempt to redefine Estonia as "Nordic" was seen in December 1999, when then Estonian foreign minister (and [President of Estonia](1581712.md) from 2006 until 2016) [Toomas Hendrik Ilves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toomas_Hendrik_Ilves) delivered a speech entitled "Estonia as a Nordic Country" to the [Swedish Institute for International Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish_Institute_for_International_Affairs),[^cite_note-248] with the potential political calculation behind it being the wish to distinguish Estonia from its more slowly progressing southern neighbours, which could have postponed early participation in European Union enlargement.[^cite_note-Mouritzen-249] [Andres Kasekamp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andres_Kasekamp) argued in 2005, that relevance of identity discussions in Baltic states decreased with their entrance into EU and NATO together, but predicted, that in the future, attractiveness of Nordic identity in Baltic states will grow and eventually, five Nordic states plus three Baltic states will become a single unit.[^cite_note-Mouritzen-249]

Other Estonian international organisation memberships include [OECD](33853117.md), [OSCE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OSCE), [WTO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WTO), [IMF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IMF), the [Council of the Baltic Sea States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_the_Baltic_Sea_States),[^cite_note-DFAT-234][^cite_note-250][^cite_note-251] and on 7 June 2019, was elected a non-permanent member of the United Nations Security Council for a two-year term that began on 1 January 2020.[^cite_note-ERR_20190607-252]
