Since the [Soviet era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic), the [relations with Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonia%E2%80%93Russia_relations) remain generally cold, even though practical co-operation has taken place in between.[^cite_note-253] Since 24 February 2022, the relations with Russia have further deteriorated due to [Russia's invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine_\(2022%E2%80%93present\)) of [Ukraine](31750.md). Estonia has very actively supported Ukraine during the war, providing highest support relative to its gross domestic product.[^cite_note-254][^cite_note-255]

### Military



Estonian soldiers during a NATO exercise in 2015

The [Estonian Defence League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Defence_League) is a voluntary national defence organisation under management of Ministry of Defence. It is organised based on military principles, has its own military equipment, and provides various different military training for its members, including in guerilla tactics. The Defence League has 17,000 members, with additional 11,000 volunteers in its affiliated organisations.[^cite_note-256][^cite_note-257]

Estonia co-operates with [Latvia](17514.md) and [Lithuania](17675.md) in several trilateral Baltic defence co-operation initiatives. As part of Baltic Air Surveillance Network (BALTNET) the three countries manage the Baltic airspace control center, Baltic Battalion (BALTBAT) has participated in the [NATO Response Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NATO_Response_Force), and a joint military educational institution [Baltic Defence College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_Defence_College) is located in [Tartu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tartu).[^cite_note-258]

Estonia joined [NATO](21133.md) on 29 March 2004.[^cite_note-259] NATO [Cooperative Cyber Defence Centre of Excellence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooperative_Cyber_Defence_Centre_of_Excellence) was established in Tallinn in 2008.[^cite_note-260] In response to [Russian war in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_military_intervention_in_Ukraine_\(2014%E2%80%93present\)), since 2017 a [NATO Enhanced Forward Presence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NATO_Enhanced_Forward_Presence) battalion battle group has been based in [Tapa Army Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tapa_Army_Base).[^cite_note-261] Also part of NATO, the [Baltic Air Policing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_Air_Policing) deployment has been based in [Ämari Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%84mari_Air_Base) since 2014.[^cite_note-262] In the European Union, Estonia participates in [Nordic Battlegroup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_Battlegroup) and [Permanent Structured Cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_Structured_Cooperation).[^cite_note-263][^cite_note-264]

According to the 2024 [Global Peace Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Peace_Index), Estonia is the 24th most peaceful country in the world.[^cite_note-265] Since 1995, Estonia has participated in numerous international security and peacekeeping missions, including: [Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Security_Assistance_Force), [Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-National_Force_%E2%80%93_Iraq), [Lebanon](787766.md), [Kosovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kosovo_Force), and [Mali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Barkhane).[^cite_note-266] The peak strength of Estonian deployment in Afghanistan was 289 soldiers in 2009.[^cite_note-267] Eleven Estonian soldiers have been killed in missions of Afghanistan and Iraq.[^cite_note-268]

### Administrative divisions

Main articles: [Administrative divisions of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_Estonia), [Counties of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counties_of_Estonia), [Municipalities of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipalities_of_Estonia), [Boroughs of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boroughs_of_Estonia), and [Populated places in Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populated_places_in_Estonia)



Administrative divisions of Estonia

Estonia is a unitary country with a single-tier local government system. Local affairs are managed autonomously by local governments. Since administrative reform in 2017, there are in total 79 local governments, including 15 towns and 64 rural municipalities. All municipalities have equal legal status and form part of a *[maakond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counties_of_Estonia)* (county), which is an administrative subunit of the state.[^cite_note-269] Representative body of local authorities is municipal council, elected at general direct elections for a four-year term. The council appoints local government. For towns, the head of the local government is *linnapea* (mayor) and *vallavanem* for parishes. For additional decentralization the local authorities may form municipal districts with limited authority, currently those have been formed in [Tallinn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallinn) and [Hiiumaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiiumaa).[^cite_note-rahandusmin-270]

Separately from administrative units, there are also [settlement units](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populated_places_in_Estonia): village, small borough, borough, and town. Generally, villages have less than 300, small boroughs have between 300 and 1000, boroughs and towns have over 1000 inhabitants.[^cite_note-rahandusmin-270]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Estonia)

As a member of the European Union and [OECD](33853117.md), Estonia is considered a high-income economy by the [World Bank](36752.md). The [GDP (PPP) per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity) of the country was \$46,385 in 2023 according to the [International Monetary Fund](15251.md), ranked 40th.[^cite_note-IMFWEO.EE-7]

Estonia ranks highly in international rankings for [quality of life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quality_of_life),[^cite_note-271] [education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_Index),[^cite_note-272] [press freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_Freedom_Index), [digitalisation of public services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E-government)[^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274] and the prevalence of technology companies.[^cite_note-275]

Beginning 1 January 2011, Estonia adopted the euro and became the 17th [eurozone](184391.md) member state.[^cite_note-euroreuters-276]

Estonia produces about 75% of its consumed electricity.[^cite_note-277] In 2011, about 85% of it was generated with locally mined [oil shale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_shale).[^cite_note-278] Alternative energy sources such as wood, peat, and biomass make up approximately 9% of primary energy production. Renewable wind energy was about 6% of total consumption in 2009.[^cite_note-279] Estonia imports [petroleum](23195.md) products from western Europe and Russia. Estonia imports 100% of its [natural gas from Russia](24648652.md).[^cite_note-280] Oil shale energy, telecommunications, textiles, chemical products, banking, services, food and fishing, timber, shipbuilding, electronics, and transportation are key sectors of the economy.[^cite_note-281] The [ice-free port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice-free_port) of [Muuga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Muuga), near Tallinn, is a modern facility featuring good transhipment capability, a high-capacity grain elevator, chill/frozen storage, and new oil tanker off-loading capabilities.[^cite_note-282]

aerial view of high rises at sunset

The central business district of [Tallinn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallinn)

Because of the [global economic recession that began in 2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_crisis_of_2007%E2%80%932010), the GDP of Estonia decreased by 1.4% in the 2nd quarter of 2008, over 3% in the 3rd quarter of 2008, and over 9% in the 4th quarter of 2008. The Estonian government made a supplementary negative budget, which was passed by [Riigikogu](794927.md). The revenue of the budget was decreased for 2008 by EEK 6.1 billion and the expenditure by EEK 3.2 billion.[^cite_note-283] In 2010, the economic situation stabilised and started a growth based on strong exports. In the fourth quarter of 2010, Estonian industrial output increased by 23% compared to the year before. The country has been experiencing economic growth ever since.[^cite_note-Stat-284]

According to Eurostat data, Estonian PPS GDP per capita stood at 67% of the EU average in 2008.[^cite_note-285] In 2017, the average monthly gross salary in Estonia was €1221.[^cite_note-StatisticsEstonia-286]

However, there are vast disparities in GDP between different areas of Estonia; currently, over half of the country's GDP is created in Tallinn.[^cite_note-BBN-287] In 2008, the GDP per capita of Tallinn stood at 172% of the Estonian average,[^cite_note-Statistics-288] which makes the per capita GDP of Tallinn as high as 115% of the European Union average, exceeding the average levels of other counties.

The unemployment rate in March 2016 was 6.4%, which is below the EU average,[^cite_note-StatisticsEstonia-286] while real GDP growth in 2011 was 8.0%,[^cite_note-Statistics_Estonia-289] five times the euro-zone average. In 2012, Estonia remained the only euro member with a budget surplus, and with a national debt of only 6%, it is one of the least indebted countries in Europe.[^cite_note-290]

In 2024, Estonia is ranked 1st in [Environmental Performance Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_Performance_Index) globally.[^cite_note-291]

### Economic indicators

Estonia's economy continues to benefit from a transparent government and policies that sustain a high level of [economic freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Index_of_Economic_Freedom), ranking 6th globally and 2nd in Europe.[^cite_note-292] The rule of [law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Estonia) remains strongly buttressed and enforced by an independent and efficient judicial system. A simplified tax system with flat rates and low indirect taxation, openness to foreign investment, and a liberal trade regime have supported the resilient and well-functioning economy.[^cite_note-taxfoundation1-293] As of May 2018, the [Ease of Doing Business Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ease_of_Doing_Business_Index) by the [World Bank Group](36752.md) places the country 16th in the world.[^cite_note-294] The strong focus on the IT sector through its [e-Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E-Estonia) program has led to much faster, simpler and efficient public services where for example filing a tax return takes less than five minutes and 98% of banking transactions are conducted through the internet.[^cite_note-295][^cite_note-296] Estonia has the 13th lowest business bribery risk in the world, according to TRACE Matrix.[^cite_note-traceinternational.org-297]

Estonia is a [developed country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country) with an advanced, [high-income economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-income_economy) that was among the fastest-growing in the EU since its entry in 2004.[^cite_note-298] The country ranks very high in the [Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_Human_Development_Index),[^cite_note-HDI-299] and compares well in measures of [economic freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Index_of_Economic_Freedom), [civil liberties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_in_the_World_\(report\)), education,[^cite_note-300] and [press freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_Freedom_Index).[^cite_note-301] Estonian citizens receive [universal health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_health_care),[^cite_note-302] [free education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_education),[^cite_note-303] and the longest paid [maternity leave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maternity_leave) in the OECD.[^cite_note-304] One of the world's most digitally-advanced societies,[^cite_note-305] in 2005 Estonia became the first state to hold elections over the [Internet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_voting_in_Estonia), and in 2014, the first state to provide [e-residency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E-residency_of_Estonia).[^cite_note-306]

### Historic development



Real GDP per capita development of Estonia, Latvia and Lithuania

In 1928, a stable currency, the *[kroon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_kroon)*, was established. It is issued by the [Bank of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_Estonia), the country's [central bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_bank). The word *kroon* (Estonian pronunciation: [\[ˈkroːn\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Estonian), "crown") is related to that of the other [Nordic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_countries) currencies (such as the [Swedish krona](16709.md) and the [Danish](236323.md) and [Norwegian krone](64149.md)). The kroon succeeded the [mark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_mark) in 1928 and was used until 1940. After Estonia regained its independence, the kroon was reintroduced in 1992.

After [restoring full independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Estonia#Regaining_independence), in the 1990s, Estonia styled itself as the "gateway between East and West" and aggressively pursued economic reform and reintegration with the West.[^cite_note-307][^cite_note-308][^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310] In 1994, applying the economic theories of [Milton Friedman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milton_Friedman), Estonia became one of the first countries to adopt a [flat tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flat_tax), with a uniform rate of 26% regardless of personal income. This rate has since been reduced several times, e.g., to 24% in 2005, 23% in 2006, and to 21% in 2008.[^cite_note-311] The [Government of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Estonia) finalised the design of [Estonian euro coins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_euro_coins) in late 2004, and adopted the euro as the country's currency on 1 January 2011, later than planned due to continued high inflation.[^cite_note-euroreuters-276][^cite_note-euro-EFP-312] A [Land Value Tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_Value_Tax) is levied which is used to fund local municipalities. It is a state-level tax, but 100% of the revenue is used to fund Local Councils. The rate is set by the Local Council within the limits of 0.1–2.5%. It is one of the most important sources of funding for municipalities.[^cite_note-Land_Tax_Reform-313] The Land Value Tax is levied on the value of the land only with improvements and buildings not considered. Very few exemptions are considered on the land value tax and even public institutions are subject to the tax.[^cite_note-Land_Tax_Reform-313] The tax has contributed to a high rate (\~90%)[^cite_note-Land_Tax_Reform-313] of owner-occupied residences within Estonia, compared to a rate of 67.4% in the United States.[^cite_note-314]

In 1999, Estonia experienced its worst year economically since it regained independence in 1991, largely because of the impact of the [1998 Russian financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Russian_financial_crisis).[^cite_note-315] Estonia joined the [WTO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization) in November 1999. With assistance from the European Union, the [World Bank](36752.md) and the [Nordic Investment Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_Investment_Bank), Estonia completed most of its preparations for European Union membership by the end of 2002. Estonia joined the [OECD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_Development) in 2010.[^cite_note-316]

### Transport

Main article: [Transport in Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Estonia)

The [Port of Tallinn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Tallinn), taking into account both cargo and passenger traffic, is one of the largest port enterprises of the [Baltic Sea](3335.md). In 2018, the enterprise was listed in [Tallinn Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallinn_Stock_Exchange). It was the first time in nearly 20 years in Estonia when a state-owned company went public in Estonia. It was also the 2nd largest [IPO](63879.md) in Nasdaq Tallinn in the number of retail investors participating. The Republic of Estonia remains the largest shareholder and holds 67% of the company.[^cite_note-317]

Owned by [AS Eesti Raudtee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eesti_Raudtee), there are many significant [railroad connections in Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transport_in_Estonia), such as [Tallinn–Narva railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallinn%E2%80%93Narva_railway), which is 209.6 km (130.2 mi) long main connection to [St. Petersburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Petersburg). The most important [highways in Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highways_in_Estonia), in other hand, includes [Narva Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narva_Highway) ([E20](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_route_E20)), [Tartu Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tartu_Highway) ([E263](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_route_E263)) and [Pärnu Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C3%A4rnu_Highway) ([E67](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_route_E67)).

The [Lennart Meri Tallinn Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lennart_Meri_Tallinn_Airport) in Tallinn is the largest airport in Estonia. A smaller airport for international flights is also located in Tartu. International flights also used to operate via Pärnu airport.

### Resources

In 2012, oil shale supplied 70% of Estonia's [total primary energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_primary_energy_supply) and accounted for 4% of Estonia's gross domestic product.[^cite_note-audit-318][^cite_note-319] Although Estonia is in general resource-poor, the land still offers a large variety of smaller resources. The country has large [oil shale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_shale) and [limestone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limestone) deposits. In addition to oil shale and limestone, Estonia also has large reserves of [phosphorite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phosphorite), [pitchblende](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uraninite), and [granite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Granite) that currently are not mined, or not mined extensively.[^cite_note-320]

Significant quantities of [rare-earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rare-earth) oxides are found in tailings accumulated from 50 years of [uranium ore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranium_ore), [shale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shale) and [loparite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loparite) mining at [Sillamäe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sillam%C3%A4e).[^cite_note-321] Because of the rising prices of rare earths, extraction of these oxides has become economically viable. The country currently exports around 3000 tonnes per annum, representing around 2% of world production.[^cite_note-322]

### Agriculture and forestry



Crops near [Aruküla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aruk%C3%BCla) in Northern Estonia

In the 1990s, agricultural significance in the economy declined sharply as large Soviet-era collective farms were dismantled and privatised. In recent years, large enterprises have once again become dominant, while smaller farms focus on niche markets, organic farming, and rural tourism. Recent years have seen an increase in Estonia's cultivated land, with approximately 1.05 million hectares of arable land and 0.24 million hectares of natural grasslands recorded by 2019.[^cite_note-323][^cite_note-324] Estonia has one of the largest average farm sizes within the EU at 62 hectares per farm, with around 78% of farmland owned by entities managing at least 100 hectares – far above the EU average of 49% for this ownership category.[^cite_note-325] Estonia ranks second in Europe, following [Austria](26964606.md), in the proportion of farmland under [organic cultivation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organic_farming).[^cite_note-326] As of 2012, Estonia had forests that covered 48% of the land.[^cite_note-327] Since at least 2009, there has been a substantial increase in logging, and logging occurs not only nationwide in private land, but even in supposedly protected land like the national park.[^cite_note-\:0-328] Estonia needs to cut significantly less forest to retain biodiversity and meet the country's carbon sequestration goal,[^cite_note-329] but it is increasing, and in 2022 the government ministry responsible for forestry, the RMK, reported a record profit of 1.4 billion euros.[^cite_note-330]

### Industry and environment

See also: [Oil shale in Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_shale_in_Estonia), [Narva Power Plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narva_Power_Plants), and [Wind power in Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_power_in_Estonia)

Rõuste wind turbines next to wetland

Rõuste wind farm in [Lääneranna Parish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%A4%C3%A4neranna_Parish)

Food, construction, and electronic industries are currently among the most important branches of Estonia's industry.[^cite_note-331] In 2007, the construction industry employed more than 80,000 people, around 12% of the entire country's workforce.[^cite_note-332] Another important industrial sector is the machinery and chemical industry, which is mainly located in [Ida-Viru county](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ida-Viru_county) and around Tallinn.

The oil shale-based [mining industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mining_industry), also concentrated in [East Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virumaa), produces around 73% of the entire country's electricity.[^cite_note-333] Although the number of pollutants emitted has been falling since the 1980s,[^cite_note-Auer-334] the air is still contaminated with [sulphur dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulphur_dioxide) from the mining industry the Soviet Union rapidly developed in the early 1950s. In some areas, coastal seawater is polluted, mainly around the [Sillamäe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sillam%C3%A4e) industrial complex.[^cite_note-335]

Estonia is dependent on other countries for energy. In recent years, many local and foreign companies have been investing in renewable energy sources.[^cite_note-336][^cite_note-337][^cite_note-338] Wind power has been increasing steadily in Estonia and the total current amount of energy produced from wind is nearly 60 [MW](21347693.md); another roughly 399 MW worth of projects are currently being developed and more than 2800 MW being proposed in the [Lake Peipus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Peipus) area and coastal areas of [Hiiumaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiiumaa).[^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340][^cite_note-341]

Currently, there are plans to renovate some older units of the Narva Power Plants, establish new power stations, and provide higher efficiency in oil shale-based energy production.[^cite_note-342] Estonia [liberalised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalization) 35% of its electricity market in April 2010; the electricity market as whole was to be liberalised by 2013.[^cite_note-343]

Together with Lithuania, Poland, and Latvia, the country considered participating in constructing the [Visaginas nuclear power plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visaginas_nuclear_power_plant) in Lithuania to replace the [Ignalina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ignalina_Nuclear_Power_Plant) nuclear plant.[^cite_note-wnn-lt-344][^cite_note-le-345] However, due to the slow pace of the project and problems with the nuclear sector, [Eesti Energia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eesti_Energia) shifted its main focus to [shale oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shale_oil) production, seen as far more profitable.[^cite_note-346]

The Estonian electricity network forms a part of the [Nord Pool Spot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nord_Pool_Spot) network.[^cite_note-347]

Estonia has a strong information technology [sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quaternary_sector_of_the_economy), partly owing to the [Tiigrihüpe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiigrih%C3%BCpe) project undertaken in the mid-1990s, and has been mentioned as the most "wired" and advanced country in Europe in the terms of e-Government of Estonia.[^cite_note-348] The 2014 [e-residency program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E-residency_of_Estonia) began offering those services to non-residents in Estonia.

[Skype](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skype) was written by Estonia-based developers [Ahti Heinla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahti_Heinla), [Priit Kasesalu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Priit_Kasesalu) and [Jaan Tallinn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaan_Tallinn), who had also originally developed [Kazaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazaa).[^cite_note-349] Other notable [startups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Startup_company) that originated from Estonia include [Bolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolt_\(company\)), [GrabCAD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GrabCAD), [Fortumo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortumo) and [Wise (formerly known as TransferWise)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TransferWise). It has been reported that Estonia has the highest startups per person ratio in the world.[^cite_note-350] As of January 2022, there are 1,291 startups from Estonia, seven of which are [unicorns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unicorn_\(finance\)), equalling nearly 1 startup per 1,000 Estonians.[^cite_note-351][^cite_note-352]

### Trade

Main article: [Tallinn Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallinn_Stock_Exchange)

Estonia has had a [market economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_economy) since the end of the 1990s and one of the highest per capita income levels in Eastern Europe.[^cite_note-353] Proximity to the Scandinavian and Finnish markets, its location between the East and West, competitive cost structure and a highly skilled labour force have been the major Estonian comparative advantages in the beginning of the 2000s (decade). As the largest city, Tallinn has emerged as a [financial centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_centre) and the [Tallinn Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallinn_Stock_Exchange) joined recently with the [OMX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OMX) system. Several cryptocurrency trading platforms are officially recognised by the government, such as [CoinMetro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CoinMetro?action=edit\&redlink=1).[^cite_note-354] The current government has pursued tight [fiscal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finance) policies, resulting in balanced budgets and low [public debt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_debt).

### Science and technology

See also: [Space science in Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_science_in_Estonia)

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Estonia)

The population of Estonia, from 1960 to 2019, with a peak in 1990.

Population of Estonia 1960–2019. The changes are largely attributed to Soviet immigration and emigration.

[^cite_note-stat.ee_2-355]

Before World War II, ethnic [Estonians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonians) made up 88% of the population, with national minorities constituting the remaining 12%.[^cite_note-ethnic_minor-356] The largest minority groups in 1934 were [Russians](59510.md), [Germans](152735.md), [Swedes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish_people), [Latvians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latvians), [Jews](25955086.md), [Poles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_people), and [Finns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finns). Other smaller minorities in Estonia are [Armenians](387816.md), [Azerbaijanis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijanis), [Moldovans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moldovans), [Chuvash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chuvash_people), [Karelians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karelians) and [Romani people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_people).[^cite_note-357]

The share of [Baltic Germans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_Germans) in Estonia had fallen from 5.3% (\~46,700) in 1881 to 1.3% (16,346) by 1934,[^cite_note-ethnic_minor-356][^cite_note-baltic_germans-358] mainly due to emigration to Germany in the light of general Russification at the end of the 19th century and the independence of Estonia in the 20th century.

Between 1945 and 1989, the share of ethnic Estonians in the population resident within the currently defined boundaries of Estonia dropped to 61%, caused primarily by the Soviet occupation and programme promoting mass immigration of urban industrial workers from Russia, Ukraine, and Belarus, as well as by wartime emigration and [Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin)'s [mass deportations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_deportations_from_Estonia) and executions.[^cite_note-359] By 1989, ethnic minorities constituted more than one-third of the population, as the number of non-Estonians had grown almost fivefold.

At the end of the 1980s, Estonians perceived their demographic change as a [national catastrophe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disaster). This was a result of the [migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_migration) policies essential to the [Sovietization program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovietization_of_the_Baltic_states), which aimed to [russify](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russification) Estonia. In the decade after the restoration of Estonian independence, large-scale emigration by ethnic Russians and the removal of Russian military bases in 1994 caused the proportion of ethnic Estonians in Estonia to increase from 61% to 69% in 2006.

Modern Estonia is a fairly ethnically homogeneous country, but this historical homogeneity is a feature of 13 of the country's 15 *maakond* (counties). The mostly Russian-speaking immigrant population is concentrated in urban areas which administratively belong to two counties. Thus 13 of Estonia's 15 counties are over 80% ethnic Estonian, the most homogeneous being [Hiiumaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiiumaa), where Estonians account for 98.4% of the population. In the counties of [Harju](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harju_County) (including the capital city Tallinn) and [Ida-Viru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ida-Viru_County), however, ethnic Estonians make up 60% and 20% of the population, respectively. The [ethnic Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Russians_in_Estonia) immigrant minority makes up about 24% of the country's total population today, but accounts for 35% of the population in Harju county and for a near-70% majority in Ida-Viru county.

The Estonian Cultural Autonomy law that was passed in 1925 was unique in Europe at that time.[^cite_note-360] Cultural autonomies could be granted to [minorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minorities) numbering more than 3,000 people with longstanding ties to the Republic of Estonia. Before the [Soviet occupation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic), the German and Jewish minorities managed to elect a cultural council. The Law on Cultural Autonomy for National Minorities was reinstated in 1993. Historically, large parts of Estonia's northwestern coast and islands have been populated by the indigenous ethnic group of *[rannarootslased](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Swedes)* ("Coastal Swedes").

In recent years, the number of Swedish residents in Estonia has risen again, numbering almost 500 people by 2008, owing to property reforms enacted in the early 1990s. In 2004, the [Ingrian Finnish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ingrian_Finnish) minority in Estonia elected a cultural council and was granted cultural autonomy. The Estonian Swedes minority similarly received cultural autonomy in 2007.[^cite_note-National_Minorities_Cultural_Autonomy_Act_(2019_version)-361] During the Russo-Ukrainian war of 2022, tens of thousands of Ukrainian refugees have arrived in Estonia.

There is also a [Roma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_people) community in Estonia. Approximately 1,000-1,500 Roma live in Estonia.[^cite_note-362]

### Society

See also: [Human rights in Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Estonia), [Nordic identity in Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_identity_in_Estonia), and [Estonian alien's passport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_alien's_passport)

The Estonian society has undergone considerable changes since the country had restored full independence in 1991.[^cite_note-363] Some of the more notable changes have taken effect in the level of stratification and distribution of family income. The [Gini coefficient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gini_coefficient) has held steadily higher than the European Union average (31 in 2009),[^cite_note-364] although it has clearly dropped. The registered unemployment rate in January 2021 was 6.9%.[^cite_note-365]

Estonia's population on 31 December 2021 (1,331,824 people) was about 3% higher than in the previous census of 2011. 84% of people residing in Estonia in 2021 lived in Estonia at the time of the previous census as well. 11% had been added by births and 5% by immigration over the ten years 2011–2021. Nowadays, 211 different self-reported ethnic groups are represented in the country's population and 243 different mother tongues are spoken. Census data indicate that Estonia has continued to stand out among European countries for its highly educated population – 43% of the population aged 25–64 have a university education, which puts Estonia in 7th place in Europe (Estonian women rank 3rd in terms of educational attainment).

More people of different ethnic origin live in Estonia than ever before, however the share of Estonians in the population has remained stable over the three censuses (2000: 68.3%; 2011: 69.8%; 2021: 69.4%). Estonian is spoken by 84% of the population: 67% of people speak it as their mother tongue and 17% as a foreign language. Compared with previous censuses, the proportion of people who speak Estonian has increased (2000: 80%; 2011: 82%), particularly due to people who have learned to speak Estonian as a foreign language (2000: 12%; 2011: 14%). It has been estimated that 76% of Estonia's population can speak a foreign language. As of 2021 census data, English is the most widely spoken foreign language in Estonia (overtaking the top position from Russian, which had still been the most widely spoken foreign language in Estonia in 2011 and earlier censuses). An estimated 17% of the native Estonian-speaking population can speak a dialect of Estonian. [^cite_note-366]

As of 2 July 2010, 84.1% of Estonian residents were Estonian citizens, 8.6% were citizens of other countries and 7.3% were "citizens with undetermined citizenship".[^cite_note-367] Since 1992, roughly 140,000 people have acquired Estonian citizenship by passing [naturalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naturalisation) exams.[^cite_note-368] Estonia has also accepted [quota refugees](46415102.md#Relocation_and_resettlement_of_asylum_seekers) under the migrant plan agreed upon by EU member states in 2015.[^cite_note-369]

Ethnic distribution in Estonia is very homogeneous at a county level; in most counties, over 90% of residents are ethnic Estonians. In contrast, in the capital city Tallinn and the urban areas of Ida-Viru county (which neighbours Russia) ethnic Estonians account for around 60% of the population and the remainder is mostly composed of Russian and Ukrainian immigrants, who mostly arrived in Estonia during the period of Soviet occupation (1944–1991), however now also includes over 62,000 (ca 6% of total population) war refugees from Ukraine who have settled in Estonia in 2022.[^cite_note-370]



A [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russians_in_Estonia) [Old Believer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Believers) village with a church on [Piirissaar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piirissaar) island

The 2008 [United Nations Human Rights Council](635790.md) report called "extremely credible" the description of the [citizenship policy of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_nationality_law) as "discriminatory".[^cite_note-371] According to surveys, only 5% of the Russian community have considered returning to Russia in the near future. Estonian Russians have developed their own identity – more than half of the respondents recognized that Estonian Russians differ noticeably from the Russians in Russia. When compared with results from a 2000 survey, Russians had a more positive attitude toward the future.[^cite_note-Societal_survey-372]

Estonia was the first former Soviet republic to legalize civil unions for same-sex couples, with a law approved in October 2014.[^cite_note-373] Political disagreements delayed adoption of the necessary implementing legislation, and same-sex couples were not able to sign [cohabitation agreements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recognition_of_same-sex_unions_in_Estonia) until January 1, 2016.

### Urbanization

For a more comprehensive list, see [List of cities and towns in Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_and_towns_in_Estonia).

Tallinn is the capital and the largest city of Estonia, and lies on the northern coast of Estonia, along the [Gulf of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Finland). There are 33 cities and several town-parish towns in the country. In total, there are 47 *linn*a, with "linn" in English meaning both "cities" and "towns". More than 70% of the population lives in towns.&#x20;
