### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Estonia)

Estonia has a diverse religious history, owing to influences from various neighboring societies. In recent years it has become increasingly secular, with either a [plurality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plurality_\(voting\)) or a [majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majority) of the population declaring themselves [nonreligious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion) in recent censuses, followed by those who identify as religiously "undeclared". The largest minority groups are the various [Christian denominations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_denomination), principally Lutheran and Orthodox Christians, with very small numbers of adherents of non-Christian faiths, namely [Judaism](15624.md), [Islam](6037917.md) and [Buddhism](3267529.md). Other polls suggest the country is broadly split between Christians and the non-religious / religiously undeclared.



Lutheran parish church at Rõuge

Before the [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War), Estonia was approximately 80% [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant), overwhelmingly [Lutheran](23371382.md).[^cite_note-IvkovićHaberfeld2015-375]

Today, Estonia's constitution guarantees freedom of religion, separation of church and state, and individual rights to privacy of belief and religion.[^cite_note-376] According to the Dentsu Communication Institute Inc, Estonia is one of the [least religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion_by_country) countries in the world, with 75.7% of the population claiming to be [irreligious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion). The [Eurobarometer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurobarometer) Poll 2005 found that only 16% of Estonians profess a belief in a god, the lowest belief of all countries studied.[^cite_note-377] A 2009 [Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup_\(company\)) poll found similar results, with only 16% of Estonians describing religion as "important" in their daily lives, making Estonia the most irreligious of the nations surveyed.[^cite_note-gallup-religiosity-poor-nations-378]

According to research, most Estonians fall within "[spiritual but not religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiritual_but_not_religious)" category. 57% of Estonians believe in a "higher spiritual force that guides the world" and 37% believe in [reincarnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reincarnation), those numbers among the highest in Europe. 84% of Estonians believe that [animals have souls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animism) and 65% believe that plants have souls as well.[^cite_note-379]

&#x20;Polls about religiosity in the [European Union](9317.md) in 2012 by [Eurobarometer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurobarometer) found that Christianity was the largest religion in Estonia accounting for 45% of [Estonians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonians).[^cite_note-EUROBAROMETER-380] [Eastern Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox) were the largest Christian group in Estonia, accounting for 17% of citizens,[^cite_note-EUROBAROMETER-380] while [Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant) made up 6%, and those identifying otherwise as Christian made up 22%. [Agnostic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agnostic) individuals were 22%, [atheists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheist) 15%, and an additional 15% did not declare a response.[^cite_note-EUROBAROMETER-380]



[Kaali meteorite crater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaali_crater) in [Saaremaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saaremaa) is considered a [sacred natural site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacred_natural_site) by the adherents of [Estonian neopaganism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_neopaganism)

A 2015 study by [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center), found that 51% of the population of Estonia declared itself to be Christian, 45% [religiously unaffiliated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion)—a category which includes [atheists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheists), agnostics and those who describe their religion as "[nothing in particular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nothing_in_Particular)", while 2% belonged to other faiths.[^cite_note-Religious_Belief_in_Central_and_Eastern_Europe-381] The Christians were divided between 25% Eastern Orthodox, 20% Lutherans, 1% Catholic and 5% other Christians.[^cite_note-382] Meanwhile, the [religiously unaffiliated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion) were divided between 9% as atheists, 1% as agnostics and 35% as believing in “[nothing in particular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nothing_In_Particular?action=edit\&redlink=1)”.[^cite_note-383]

Traditionally, the largest religious denomination in the country was [Lutheranism](23371382.md), which was adhered to by 160,000 Estonians (or 13% of the population) according to the 2000 census, principally ethnic Estonians. According to the Lutheran World Federation, the historic Lutheran denomination has 180,000 registered members.[^cite_note-384] Other organisations, such as the World Council of Churches, report that there are as many as 265,700 Estonian Lutherans.[^cite_note-385] Additionally, there are between 8,000 and 9,000 members abroad. However, the 2011 census indicated that [Eastern Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox) had surpassed Lutheranism, accounting for 16.5% of the population (176,773 people). While not being a [state church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_religion), the Lutheran church had an agreement giving preferential status to the Lutheran church until year 2023.[^cite_note-386]

Eastern Orthodoxy is practised chiefly by the ethnic Russian minority, as well as by the small ethnic Estonian and [Seto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Setos) minority. The [Estonian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Orthodox_Church_of_Moscow_Patriarchate), affiliated with the [Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church), is the primary Orthodox denomination. The [Estonian Apostolic Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Apostolic_Orthodox_Church), under the Greek-Orthodox [Ecumenical Patriarchate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarchate), claims another 28,000 members.

Catholics are a small minority in Estonia. They are organised under the Latin [Apostolic Administration of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_Administration_of_Estonia) and two Greek Catholic parishes.

According to the census of 2000 (data in table to the right), there were about 1,000 adherents of the [Taara faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taaraism)[^cite_note-387][^cite_note-388][^cite_note-389] or [Maausk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maausk) in Estonia (see [Maavalla Koda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maavalla_Koda)). The Jewish community has an estimated population of about 1,900 (see [History of the Jews in Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Estonia)), and the Muslim community numbers just over 1,400. Around 68,000 people consider themselves [atheists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheists).[^cite_note-pub.stat.ee-390]

### Languages

Main article: [Languages of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Estonia)



Distribution of [Finnic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnic_languages) in [Northern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Europe)

The official language, [Estonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_language), is a [Finnic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnic_languages) language, and is conventionally classified as a member of the [Uralic language family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uralic_languages), thus and one of the few languages of Europe that is not of [Indo-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_language) origin. The Estonian language is the world's second-most spoken Finnic language as well as the world's third-most spoken Uralic language (after [Hungarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_language) and [Finnish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_language)).

Although the Estonian and [Germanic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanic_language) are of different origins, one can identify many similar words in Estonian and German. This is primarily because the Estonian language has borrowed nearly one-third of its vocabulary from [Germanic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanic_languages), mainly from Low Saxon ([Middle Low German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Low_German)) during the period of [German rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Estonia#Estonian_Crusade:_The_Middle_Ages), and [High German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_German) (including [standard German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_German)). The percentage of Low Saxon and High German loanwords can be estimated at 22–25 percent, with Low Saxon making up about 15 percent.

[South Estonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Estonian) languages are spoken by 100,000 people and include the dialects of [Võro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%B5ro_language) and [Seto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seto_language). The languages are spoken in South-Eastern Estonia and are [genealogically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_relationship_\(linguistics\)) distinct from northern Estonian, but are traditionally and officially considered as [dialects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dialect) and "regional forms of the Estonian language", not separate language(s).[^cite_note-391]

Russian is the most spoken minority language in the country. There are towns in Estonia with large concentrations of Russian speakers, and there are towns where Estonian speakers are in the minority (especially in the northeast, e.g. [Narva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narva)). Russian is spoken as a secondary language by many 40- to 70-year-old ethnic Estonians because Russian was the unofficial language of the [Estonian SSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic) from 1944 to 1990 and was taught as a compulsory second language during the Soviet era. In the period between 1990 and 1995, the Russian language was granted an official special status according to Estonian language laws.[^cite_note-392] In 1995 it lost its official status. In 1998, most first- and second-generation industrial immigrants from the former Soviet Union (mainly the [Russian SFSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Soviet_Federative_Socialist_Republic)) did not speak Estonian.[^cite_note-393] However, by 2010, 64.1% of non-ethnic Estonians spoke Estonian.[^cite_note-394] The latter, mostly Russian-speaking ethnic minorities, reside predominantly in the capital city of Tallinn and the industrial urban areas in [Ida-Viru county](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ida-Viru_County).

From the 13th to the 20th century, there were Swedish-speaking communities in Estonia, particularly in the coastal areas and on the islands, which today have almost disappeared. From 1918 to 1940, when Estonia was independent, the small Swedish community was well treated. Municipalities with a Swedish majority, mainly found along the coast, used Swedish as the administrative language and Swedish-Estonian culture saw an upswing. However, most Swedish-speaking people fled to Sweden before the invasion of Estonia by the Soviet army in 1944. Only a handful of older speakers remain.[^cite_note-395][^cite_note-396] Lotfitka Romani is spoken by the [Roma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_people) minority in Estonia.[^cite_note-397]

[English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language) is the most widely spoken foreign language in Estonia today. According to the most recent (2021) census data 76% of the population can speak a foreign language. After English, Russian is the second most widely spoken foreign language in Estonia, and in the census 17% of the native speakers of standard Estonian reported that they can also speak a dialect of Estonian.[^cite_note-398][^cite_note-PopulationByNationality-190] The most common foreign languages learned by Estonian students are English, Russian, German, and French. Other popular languages include Finnish, Spanish, and Swedish.[^cite_note-399]

### Education and science

gray stucco building three-story building with grey slate hip roof, central portico and pediment

The [University of Tartu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Tartu) is one of the oldest universities in Northern Europe and the highest-ranked university in Estonia. According to the Top Universities website, the University of Tartu ranks 285th in the QS Global World Ranking.

[^cite_note-400]

Main article: [Education in Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Estonia)

See also: [List of universities in Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_universities_in_Estonia), [Space science in Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_science_in_Estonia), and [Tiigrihüpe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiigrih%C3%BCpe)

The history of formal education in Estonia dates back to the 13th and 14th centuries when the first [monastic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monastic) and [cathedral schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_schools) were founded.[^cite_note-401] The first primer in the Estonian language was published in 1575. The oldest university is the [University of Tartu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Tartu), founded by King [Gustav II Adolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustavus_Adolphus_of_Sweden) of Sweden in 1632. In 1919, university courses were first taught in the Estonian language. From 2024 all schools will begin to transition to educating solely in the Estonian language.[^cite_note-402]

Today's education in Estonia is divided into general, vocational, and hobby. The education system is based on four levels: pre-school, basic, secondary, and higher education.[^cite_note-403] A wide network of schools and supporting educational institutions have been established. The Estonian education system consists of state, municipal, public, and private institutions. There are currently (in 2009) 589 schools in Estonia.[^cite_note-404]

Estonia started connecting all its schools to the Internet very early. [Tiigrihüpe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiigrih%C3%BCpe) (Estonian for Tiger Leap) was a project undertaken by the state to heavily invest in the development and expansion of computer and network infrastructure in Estonia, with a particular emphasis on education.[^cite_note-405]

In the 2018 [Program for International Student Assessment (PISA)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Programme_for_International_Student_Assessment) report, Estonia's students rank 1st in Europe. In the world, Estonia's students rank 5th in reading, 8th in mathematics and 4th in sciences.[^cite_note-LoC-406][^cite_note-407] Additionally, around 89% of Estonian adults aged 25–64 have earned the equivalent of a high-school degree, one of the highest rates in the industrialized world.[^cite_note-408]



House of the [Estonian Students' Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Students'_Society), built-in 1901–1902 in Tartu, and considered the first example of the Estonian [style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architectural_style) of urban architecture.

[^cite_note-409]

Academic higher education in Estonia is divided into three levels: bachelor's, master's, and doctoral studies. In some specialties, the bachelor's and master's levels are integrated into one unit.[^cite_note-EurydiceSummary-410] Estonian public universities have significantly more autonomy than applied higher education institutions. In addition to organizing the academic life of the university, universities can create new curricula, establish admission terms and conditions, approve the budget, approve the development plan, elect the rector, and make restricted decisions in matters concerning assets.[^cite_note-411] Estonia has a moderate number of public and private universities. The largest public universities are the [University of Tartu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Tartu), [Tallinn University of Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallinn_University_of_Technology), [Tallinn University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallinn_University), [Estonian University of Life Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_University_of_Life_Sciences), [Estonian Academy of Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Academy_of_Arts); the largest private university is [Estonian Business School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Business_School).

ESTCube-1 micro satellite orbiting globe and beaming light to Estonia

[ESTCube-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESTCube-1) is the first Estonian satellite.

The [Estonian Academy of Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Academy_of_Sciences) is the [national academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_academy) of science. The strongest public non-profit research institute that carries out fundamental and applied research is the [National Institute of Chemical Physics and Biophysics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Institute_of_Chemical_Physics_and_Biophysics) (NICPB; Estonian KBFI). The first computer centers were established in the late 1950s in Tartu and Tallinn. Estonian specialists contributed in the development of software engineering standards for ministries of the Soviet Union during the 1980s.[^cite_note-Kalja-412][^cite_note-Jaakkola-413] As of 2015, Estonia spends around 1.5% of its GDP on [Research and Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_and_Development), compared to an EU average of around 2.0%.[^cite_note-414] Estonia was ranked 16th in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index) in 2024.[^cite_note-415]

Some of the best-known scientists related to Estonia include astronomers [Friedrich Georg Wilhelm von Struve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Georg_Wilhelm_von_Struve), [Ernst Öpik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernst_%C3%96pik) and [Jaan Einasto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaan_Einasto), biologist [Karl Ernst von Baer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Ernst_von_Baer), [Jakob von Uexküll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jakob_von_Uexk%C3%BCll), chemists [Wilhelm Ostwald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilhelm_Ostwald) and [Carl Schmidt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Schmidt_\(chemist\)), economist [Ragnar Nurkse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ragnar_Nurkse), mathematician [Edgar Krahn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edgar_Krahn), medical researchers [Ludvig Puusepp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludvig_Puusepp) and [Nikolay Pirogov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolay_Pirogov), physicists [Georg Wilhelm Richmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georg_Wilhelm_Richmann) and [Thomas Johann Seebeck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Johann_Seebeck), political scientist [Rein Taagepera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rein_Taagepera), psychologist [Endel Tulving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endel_Tulving) and [Risto Näätänen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Risto_N%C3%A4%C3%A4t%C3%A4nen), semiotician [Juri Lotman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juri_Lotman).

According to *New Scientist*, Estonia will be the first nation to provide personal genetic information services sponsored by the state. They aim to minimize and prevent future ailments for those whose genes make them extra prone to conditions like adult-onset diabetes and cardiovascular diseases. The government plans to provide lifestyle advice based on the DNA for 100,000 of its 1.3 million citizens.[^cite_note-416]

Estonia is a member of the international scientific organisations CERN,[^cite_note-417] ESA,[^cite_note-autogenerated1-418] and UNESCO.

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Estonia)

See also: [List of Estonians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Estonians)



The [Estonian National Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_National_Museum) in Tartu

The [culture of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Estonia) incorporates indigenous heritage, as represented by the Estonian language and the [sauna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sauna), with mainstream [Nordic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_countries) and European cultural aspects. Because of its history and geography, Estonia's culture has been influenced by the traditions of the adjacent area's various Finnic, Baltic, Slavic and Germanic peoples as well as the cultural developments in the former dominant powers Germany, Sweden and Russia, for this reason it aspires more to be [considered a Nordic state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_identity_in_Estonia).[^cite_note-419][^cite_note-420]

Today, Estonian society encourages liberty and liberalism, with a popular commitment to the ideals of the limited government, discouraging centralised power and corruption. The [Protestant work ethic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_work_ethic) remains a significant cultural staple, and free education is a highly prized institution. As the mainstream culture in the Nordic countries, Estonian culture can be seen to build upon the ascetic environmental realities and traditional livelihoods, a heritage of comparatively widespread [egalitarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egalitarianism) out of practical reasons (see: [Everyman's right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everyman's_right) and [universal suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_suffrage)), and the ideals of closeness to nature and [self-sufficiency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-sufficiency) (see: [summer cottage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Summer_cottage)).

The [Estonian Academy of Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Academy_of_Arts) (Estonian: *Eesti Kunstiakadeemia*, EKA) is providing higher education in art, design, architecture, media, art history and conservation while the [University of Tartu Viljandi Culture Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Tartu_Viljandi_Culture_Academy) has an approach to popularise native culture through such curricula as native construction, native blacksmithing, native textile design, traditional handicraft and traditional music, but also jazz and church music. In 2010, there were 245 museums in Estonia whose combined collections contain more than 10 million objects.[^cite_note-421]

### Music

Main article: [Music of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Estonia)

See also: [Estonian national awakening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_national_awakening), [Estonian Song Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Song_Festival), and [Estonia in the Eurovision Song Contest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonia_in_the_Eurovision_Song_Contest)



The [Estonian Song Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Song_Festival) is [UNESCO](21786641.md)'s [Masterpiece of the Oral and Intangible Heritage of Humanity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masterpieces_of_the_Oral_and_Intangible_Heritage_of_Humanity).

The earliest mention of Estonian singing dates back to [Saxo Grammaticus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saxo_Grammaticus) *Gesta Danorum* (c. 1179).[^cite_note-422] Saxo speaks of Estonian warriors who sang at night while waiting for a battle. The older [folk songs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_music) are also referred to as *regilaulud*, songs in the traditional *regivärss* [poetic metre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metre_\(poetry\)) shared by all [Baltic Finns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_Finns). Runic singing was widespread among Estonians until the 18th century, when rhythmic folk songs began to replace them.[^cite_note-423]

Traditional wind instruments derived from those used by [shepherds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shepherd) were once widespread, and are now becoming more commonly played once more. Other instruments, including the [fiddle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiddle), [zither](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zither), [concertina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concertina), and [accordion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accordion) are used to play [polka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polka) or other dance music. The [kannel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kannel_\(instrument\)) is a native instrument that is again becoming more popular in Estonia. A Native Music Preserving Centre was opened in 2008 in [Viljandi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viljandi).[^cite_note-424]

Arvo Pärt bearded balding man facing left

[Arvo Pärt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arvo_P%C3%A4rt) was the world's most performed living composer from 2010 to 2018.

The tradition of [Estonian Song Festivals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Song_Festival) (*Laulupidu*) started at the height of the [Estonian national awakening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_national_awakening) in 1869. Today, it is one of the largest amateur [choral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Choral) events in the world. In 2004, about 100,000 people participated in the Song Festival. Since 1928, [the Tallinn Song Festival Grounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Tallinn_Song_Festival_Grounds) (*Lauluväljak*) have hosted the event every five years in July. The last festival took place in July 2025. In addition, Youth Song Festivals are also held every four or five years, the latest taking place in 2017.[^cite_note-425]

Professional Estonian musicians and composers such as [Aleksander Eduard Thomson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleksander_Eduard_Thomson), [Rudolf Tobias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudolf_Tobias), [Miina Härma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miina_H%C3%A4rma), [Mart Saar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mart_Saar), [Artur Kapp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artur_Kapp), [Juhan Aavik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juhan_Aavik), [Aleksander Kunileid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleksander_Kunileid), [Artur Lemba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artur_Lemba) and [Heino Eller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heino_Eller) emerged in the late 19th century. Currently, the most well-known Estonian composers are [Arvo Pärt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arvo_P%C3%A4rt), [Eduard Tubin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eduard_Tubin), and [Veljo Tormis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veljo_Tormis).[^cite_note-426] In 2014, Arvo Pärt was the world's most performed living composer for the fourth year in a row.[^cite_note-427]

In the 1950s, Estonian [baritone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baritone) [Georg Ots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georg_Ots) rose to worldwide prominence.[^cite_note-428] In popular music, Estonian artist [Kerli Kõiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerli_K%C3%B5iv) has become popular in Europe, also gaining in popularity in North America.

Estonia won the [Eurovision Song Contest](9954.md) in 2001 with the song "[Everybody](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everybody_\(Eurovision_song\))" performed by [Tanel Padar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanel_Padar) and [Dave Benton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dave_Benton). In 2002, Estonia hosted the event. [Maarja-Liis Ilus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maarja-Liis_Ilus) competed for Estonia in 1996 and 1997, while [Eda-Ines Etti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eda-Ines_Etti), [Koit Toome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koit_Toome) and [Evelin Samuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evelin_Samuel) partly owe their popularity to the song contest. [Lenna Kuurmaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lenna_Kuurmaa) gained recognition in Europe performing with her band [Vanilla Ninja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vanilla_Ninja). "[Rändajad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C3%A4ndajad)" by [Urban Symphony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_Symphony) was the first song in Estonian to chart in the UK, Belgium and Switzerland.

Estonian country guitar player [Laur Joamets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laur_Joamets?action=edit\&redlink=1) won a [Grammy Award](44635.md) with the country singer [Sturgill Simpson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sturgill_Simpson) in 2017 for the Best Country Record of the year, [A Sailor's Guide to Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Sailor's_Guide_to_Earth).[^cite_note-429]

### Art

Traces of Estonian artistry date back to the Stone Age, with decorated bone artefacts, [amber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amber) pendants, and early figurines. During the Middle Ages, [Gothic art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_art) became prominent, visible in the medieval churches of Saaremaa and exemplified by [Bernt Notke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernt_Notke)'s *[Danse Macabre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danse_Macabre_\(Notke\))* in [St. Nicholas Church, Tallinn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Nicholas_Church,_Tallinn).[^cite_note-430] Renaissance painter [Michael Sittow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Sittow), trained in the [Early Netherlandish style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Netherlandish_painting), was Estonia's first internationally recognised artist.[^cite_note-431]



[Gustav Adolf Hippius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustav_Adolf_Hippius), *An Estonian bride*, 1852, [Art Museum of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Museum_of_Estonia), Tallinn

In the Neoclassical period, landscape painting gained prominence among Estonian artists, both at home and abroad. This era also saw the emergence of other genres like mural painting, miniature painting, glass painting, and watercolours, often featuring antique themes or Estonian nature scenes. Famous painters of this era include [Gustav Adolf Hippius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustav_Adolf_Hippius), [Karl August Senff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_August_Senff) and [Julie Wilhelmine Hagen-Schwarz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julie_Wilhelmine_Hagen-Schwarz).[^cite_note-432][^cite_note-433] The 19th and early 20th centuries saw also a rise in national themes, led by painters like [Johann Köler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_K%C3%B6ler), who embraced Estonian landscapes and traditions. [August Weizenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/August_Weizenberg) is considered one of the founders of Estonian sculpture. His eclectic style, primarily based on classicism, favoured marble. Another Estonian sculptor, [Amandus Adamson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amandus_Adamson), shifted towards a more relaxed style that incorporated elements of realism. [Anton Starkopf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anton_Starkopf), a prominent sculptor of the 1930s, developed a unique style using granite and explored diverse themes, including erotic undertones. The Soviet occupation halted the progress of sculpture in the 1940s. However, since the 1960s Estonian sculpture was able to increase output and use innovative approaches again.[^cite_note-434] During the 1918–1940 period, artists increasingly integrated contemporary European avant-garde influences. The founding of the [Pallas Art School (1919–1940)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pallas_Art_School_\(1919%E2%80%931940\)) in Tartu marked a turning point, giving rise to a generation of artists including [Konrad Mägi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konrad_M%C3%A4gi), [Nikolai Triik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolai_Triik), [Kristjan Raud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kristjan_Raud), and printmaker [Eduard Wiiralt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eduard_Wiiralt), whose works captured both the avant-garde spirit and uniquely Estonian sensibilities.[^cite_note-435] When the Soviet army occupied Estonia in 1944, many Estonian artists fled westwards. Under the [Stalinist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stalinism) regime, art was heavily regulated, with [Socialist Realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Realism) promoted as the official style, while Western influences were discouraged. However, by the 1960s, restrictions began to be relaxed. A breakthrough came with the formation of the [ANK '64](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ANK_'64) collective, a group of artists who broke from Soviet themes and embraced personal, fantastical worlds. Leading figures such as [Jüri Arrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%BCri_Arrak) and [Tõnis Vint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C3%B5nis_Vint) explored modernist aesthetics, emphasising individual expression and imaginative realism. In 1966, the first Estonian modernist art works got the official permission from the Soviet authorities to be displayed in public exhibitions. By the 1970s, Estonian art had grown distinct from Moscow's official styles, embracing a modernism that highlighted personal vision and cultural identity.[^cite_note-436] Since the 1990s, Estonian art has diversified significantly with the rise of photography, video, and conceptual art. This period saw the decline of centralised art funding and management, alongside the establishment of new media centres at the Estonian Academy of Arts. Artists such as [Toomas Vint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toomas_Vint) became known internationally, and Estonian video artists gained exposure in venues like the [São Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Paulo_Art_Biennial) and [Venice Biennales](301876.md).[^cite_note-437]

### Literature

Main article: [Literature of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literature_of_Estonia)

See also: [Estophile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estophile)

[Estonian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_literature) refers to literature written in the [Estonian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_language) (ca. 1 million speakers).[^cite_note-438] The domination of Estonia after the [Northern Crusades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Crusades), from the 13th century to 1918 by Germany, Sweden, and Russia, resulted in few early literary works being written in the Estonian language. The oldest records of written Estonian date from the 13th century. *Originates Livoniae* in the [Chronicle of Henry of Livonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chronicle_of_Henry_of_Livonia) contains Estonian place names, words and sentence fragments. The *[Liber Census Daniae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liber_Census_Daniae)* (1241) contains Estonian place and family names.[^cite_note-DWE-439] Many folk tales are told to this day and some have been written down and translated to make them accessible to an international readership.[^cite_note-440] *[ABD ehk Luggemise-Ramat Lastele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABD_ehk_Luggemise-Ramat_Lastele)*, an Estonian-language [alphabet book](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alphabet_book) by [Otto Wilhelm Masing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_Wilhelm_Masing), was published in 1795.[^cite_note-441][^cite_note-442]

The cultural stratum of Estonian was originally characterised by a largely lyrical form of folk poetry based on syllabic quantity. Apart from a few, albeit remarkable, exceptions, this archaic form has not been widely employed in later times. One of the most outstanding achievements in the field is the national epic *[Kalevipoeg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalevipoeg)*. At a professional level, the traditional folk song reached its new heyday during the last quarter of the 20th century, primarily thanks to the work of composer [Veljo Tormis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veljo_Tormis).

[Oskar Luts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oskar_Luts) was the most prominent prose writer of early Estonian literature and is still widely read today, particularly his lyrical school novel *Kevade* (Spring).[^cite_note-443] [A. H. Tammsaare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._H._Tammsaare)'s social epic and psychological realist [pentalogy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentalogy), *[Truth and Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truth_and_Justice)*, captured the evolution of Estonian society from a poor farmer community to an independent nation.[^cite_note-444][^cite_note-445] In modern times, [Jaan Kross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaan_Kross) and [Jaan Kaplinski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaan_Kaplinski) are Estonia's best-known and most-translated writers.[^cite_note-446] Among the most popular writers of the late 20th and early 21st centuries are [Tõnu Õnnepalu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C3%B5nu_%C3%95nnepalu) and [Andrus Kivirähk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrus_Kivir%C3%A4hk), who uses elements of Estonian folklore and mythology, deforming them into the absurd and [grotesque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grotesque).[^cite_note-447]

### Media

Main article: [Media of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Estonia)

See also: [List of Estonian films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Estonian_films) and [List of Estonian war films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Estonian_war_films)

The [cinema of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Estonia) started in 1908 with the production of a newsreel about Swedish King [Gustav V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustav_V_of_Sweden)'s visit to Tallinn.[^cite_note-448] The first public TV broadcast in Estonia was in July 1955. Regular, live radio broadcasts began in December 1926. Deregulation in the field of electronic media has brought radical changes compared to the beginning of the 1990s. The first licences for private TV broadcasters were issued in 1992. The first private radio station went on the air in 1990.

The most internationally known Estonian films include *[Those Old Love Letters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Those_Old_Love_Letters)*, *[The Heart of the Bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Heart_of_the_Bear)*, *[Names in Marble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_in_Marble_\(film\))*, *[The Singing Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Singing_Revolution)*, *[Autumn Ball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autumn_Ball)*, *[1944](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1944_\(film\))*, *[The Fencer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Fencer)* and *[November](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/November_\(2017_film\))*. Internationally known Estonian film actors include [Lembit Ulfsak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lembit_Ulfsak), [Jaan Tätte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaan_T%C3%A4tte), and [Elmo Nüganen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elmo_N%C3%BCganen), who also known as a film director.&#x20;

Estonian media sector has a large number of weekly newspapers and magazines, and Estonians have a choice of nine domestic TV channels and a host of radio stations. Estonia has been internationally recognised for its high rate of press freedom, having been ranked 3rd in the 2012 [Press Freedom Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_Freedom_Index) by [Reporters Without Borders](526054.md).[^cite_note-449]

Estonia has two news agencies. The [Baltic News Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_News_Service) (BNS), founded in 1990, is a private regional news agency covering Estonia, Latvia and Lithuania. The ETV24 is an agency owned by *[Eesti Rahvusringhääling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eesti_Rahvusringh%C3%A4%C3%A4ling)* which is a publicly funded radio and television organisation created on 30 June 2007 to take over the functions of the formerly separate [Eesti Raadio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eesti_Raadio) and [Eesti Televisioon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eesti_Televisioon) under the terms of the Estonian National Broadcasting Act.[^cite_note-450][^cite_note-451] According to [Reporters Without Borders](526054.md), in 2024, Estonia ranked 6th in the world for press freedom, right below that of the neighboring [Finland](10577.md) (5th place).[^cite_note-452] In 2023, Estonia ranked 8th in the world for press freedom.[^cite_note-453]

### Architecture

Main article: [Architecture of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Estonia)



A traditional farmhouse built in the [Estonian vernacular style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_vernacular_architecture)

The architectural history of Estonia mainly reflects its contemporary development in northern Europe. Worth mentioning is especially the architectural ensemble that makes out the medieval old town of Tallinn, which is on the UNESCO World Heritage List.[^cite_note-454] In addition, the country has several unique, more or less preserved [hill forts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hill_fort) dating from pre-Christian times,[^cite_note-455][^cite_note-456] a large number of still intact medieval castles and churches,[^cite_note-457][^cite_note-458][^cite_note-459] while the countryside is still shaped by the presence of a vast number of wooden manor houses from earlier centuries.

### Holidays

Main article: [Public holidays in Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holidays_in_Estonia)

The Estonian [National Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Day) is the [Independence Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_\(Estonia\)) celebrated on 24 February, the day the [Estonian Declaration of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Declaration_of_Independence) was issued. As of 2013, there are 12 public holidays (which come with a day off) and 12 national holidays celebrated annually.[^cite_note-460][^cite_note-461] The following are holidays that mean days off:

| Date | English Name | Estonian Name | Remarks |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 January | [New Year's Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Year) | *uusaasta* |  |
| 24 February | [Independence Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_\(Estonia\)) | *iseseisvuspäev* | **[National Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Day)** – celebrates the [Declaration of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Declaration_of_Independence) in 1918. |
| *Moveable Friday* | [Good Friday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_Friday) | *suur reede* |  |
| *Moveable Sunday* | [Easter Sunday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Easter_Sunday) | *ülestõusmispühade 1. püha* | Commonly known as *lihavõtted* or *munadepühad*. |
| 1 May | [Spring Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spring_Day) | *kevadpüha* |  |
| *Moveable Sunday* | [Whit Sunday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whit_Sunday) | *nelipühade 1. püha* |  |
| 23 June | [Victory Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%B5idup%C3%BCha) | *Võidupüha* | Celebrates the victory in the [Battle of Võnnu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_C%C4%93sis_\(1919\)) during the [Estonian War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_War_of_Independence). |
| 24 June | [Saint John's Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_John's_Day_\(Estonia\)) | *Jaanipäev* | [Jaaniõhtu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_John's_Day_\(Estonia\)) is celebrated in the night before Midsummer Day. |
| 20 August | [Independence Restoration Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Restoration_of_Independence) | *taasiseseisvumispäev* | Celebrates Estonia's [Restoration of Independence](#Restoration_of_Independence) in 1991. |
| 24 December | [Christmas Eve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christmas_Eve) | *jõululaupäev* |  |
| 25 December | [Christmas Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christmas_Day) | *esimene jõulupüha* |  |
| 26 December | [Second Day of Christmas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Day_of_Christmas) | *teine jõulupüha* |  |

### Cuisine

Main article: [Estonian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_cuisine)

See also: [Kama (food)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kama_\(food\)), [Kalev (confectioner)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalev_\(confectioner\)), [Kohuke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kohuke), and [Verivorst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Verivorst)

Historically, the cuisine of Estonia has been dependent on seasons and the simple food from the local farms and the sea. Today, it also includes many "global" foods. The most typical foods in modern Estonia are black bread, pork, potatoes, and dairy products.[^cite_note-462] Traditionally in summer and spring, Estonians like to eat everything fresh – berries, herbs, vegetables, and everything else that comes straight from the garden. Hunting and fishing have also been very common, although currently hunting and fishing are enjoyed mostly as hobbies. Today, it is also very popular to grill outside in summer.

A cardamom-spiced bread roll with almond paste *[vastlakukkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semla)* is a traditional Estonian [sweet roll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweet_roll), especially popular from Christmas to Easter.[^cite_note-463]

Traditionally in winter, jams, preserves, and pickles are brought to the table. Gathering and preserving fruits, mushrooms, and vegetables for winter has always been popular, but today gathering and preserving is becoming less common because everything can be bought from stores. However, preparing food for winter is still very popular in the countryside.

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Estonia)

After declaring independence from Russia in 1918, Estonia first competed as an independent nation at the [1920 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1920_Summer_Olympics). Estonian athletes took part in the 1952–1988 Olympic Games under the Soviet flag, as the country had been occupied and annexed by the Soviet Union in 1940. The [1980 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Summer_Olympics) [Sailing regatta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sailing) was held in the capital city [Tallinn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallinn). After regaining independence in 1991, Estonia has participated in all Olympics. Estonia has won most of its medals in [athletics](194904.md), [weightlifting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_weightlifting), [wrestling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wrestling), and [cross-country skiing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross-country_skiing_\(sport\)). Estonia has been one of the most successful nations at the Olympics in terms of medals won per capita.[^cite_note-464] Estonia's best results were being ranked 13th in the total medals' table at the [1936 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1936_Summer_Olympics_medal_table), and 12th at the [2006 Winter Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Winter_Olympics_medal_table).

Estonia has many indoor and outdoor facilities dedicated to various sports branches.[^cite_note-465]

[Kiiking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiiking), a relatively new sport, was invented in 1993 by Ado Kosk in Estonia. Kiiking involves a modified swing in which the rider of the swing tries to go around 360 degrees.[^cite_note-466]
